If you have heard the 'Pathétique' (from B159 Ch 20), compare its opening to the opening of 'Winter Dreams.' What can the young composer already do, and what has he not yet learned?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Jansons / Oslo recording (or one of the alternatives in §3.5) and listen again to the first two movements, attending to these moments:

  1. **The shimmer and the winter-journey tune (first movement, 0:00).** Confirm the soft rapid string rustle and the thin flute-and-bassoon melody over it. Register the cold, traveling quality.

  2. **The oboe's long melody (slow movement, around 1:15).** The chapter's central moment. Follow the wide singing line, its rise and breath and fall. This is the orchestral Tchaikovsky tune in its first full form.

  3. **The slow movement's climax (around 6:30).** Listen for the melody's fullest, most ardent statement and then the slow, careful descent back to stillness. Notice that Tchaikovsky does not cut the intensity off but lets it ebb.

  4. **The folk-dance energy of the finale.** If you hear the whole symphony, listen for the moment the slow, dark finale-opening breaks into vigorous dance, and for the bright G-major arrival at the end. The winter journey ends in warmth.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the slow movement of "Winter Dreams" with the slow movement of the Fifth Symphony (the famous _Andante cantabile_ with the great horn solo), which you will reach in Chapter 14. The exercise takes about twenty-five minutes and shows you the same idea, a long Russian song for the orchestra, twenty-two years apart, in its early and its mature forms. You do not need to analyze anything; just hear how much richer, longer-breathed, and more harmonically adventurous the later song is.

**Read** : the chapters on the formation and the early Moscow years in Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (Oxford, 2009) and Anthony Holden's _Tchaikovsky: A Biography_ (1995). Wiley is precise about the Conservatory training and the development of Tchaikovsky's craft; Holden is vivid about the family, the mother's death, and the nervous crisis of 1866. Between them you will get both the technical and the human sides of how a law clerk became a composer. For the primary-source flavor, Rosa Newmarch's 1906 English abridgment of Modest Tchaikovsky's biography gives the brother's account of the childhood and the early years, to be read with awareness that Modest was shaping a posthumous image.

---

## Chapter 4: Moscow Conservatory and the First Mature Masterwork 1866-1869

Three years after he arrived in Moscow to teach harmony, Tchaikovsky wrote the first piece that the whole world still plays exactly as he would want it heard. The "Winter Dreams" symphony of Chapter 3 is a lovely, flawed apprentice work; you hear the seams. The Romeo and Juliet Fantasy-Overture has no seams. It is the first fully achieved Tchaikovsky, the first work in which the melodic gift and the structural craft lock together, and it contains a love-theme that has become, with the openings of a handful of other works, one of the most recognizable melodies ever written. This chapter is about how he got there: the daily grind of teaching, the meeting with a domineering older composer who told him what to write and how, and the eleven-year process of revising a single twenty-minute piece until it was right.

The chapter also introduces a relationship that will shadow the whole middle of the book: Tchaikovsky's uneasy connection to the Russian nationalist composers, the Mighty Five, of whom Mily Balakirev, the man who suggested Romeo and Juliet, was the leader. Chapter 5 develops that relationship in full. Here we meet it through one mentorship, generative and difficult at once, that produced a masterpiece.

### 4.1 The harmony teacher

Tchaikovsky taught harmony at the Moscow Conservatory from 1866 to 1878, twelve years, a substantial teaching load on top of his composing. He was, by the accounts of his students, a conscientious and kind teacher, if not an inspired one; he found the work draining and resented the time it took from composition, but he needed the salary and the security. He wrote a harmony textbook that was used in Russian conservatories for decades. The image to hold is not of a free artist but of a working professional: a man who taught a full schedule, lived modestly, and composed in the hours he could find, often in the summers away from Moscow.

This matters because it corrects a Romantic fantasy. Tchaikovsky did not compose in a fever of inspiration whenever the muse struck. He composed like a professional, on a schedule, treating composition as work to be done daily whether he felt inspired or not. He once wrote, in a much-quoted letter, that inspiration is a guest who does not visit the lazy, that one must work and not wait to be in the mood. The fluency and abundance of his output, symphonies and operas and ballets and concertos in such number, rests on this professional discipline. The melodies sound spontaneous; the working life that produced them was anything but.

### 4.2 Mily Balakirev and the commission of a masterpiece

In 1869 Tchaikovsky, twenty-nine, came into close contact with Mily Balakirev, the leader of the St. Petersburg nationalist circle. Balakirev was a forceful, opinionated, and musically gifted man who liked to direct other composers' work in detail, and he took an interest in the young Muscovite. It was Balakirev who suggested that Tchaikovsky write an orchestral piece on Shakespeare's _Romeo and Juliet_ , and Balakirev did far more than suggest the subject. He proposed the overall plan, recommended specific keys, criticized drafts in long letters, and pushed Tchaikovsky to revise. The opening Friar Laurence music, in particular, went through several versions at Balakirev's urging.

This is the first of the book's mentorship-and-friction stories, and it is worth seeing clearly, because it sets the pattern. Balakirev's interference was real and sometimes overbearing; he treated Tchaikovsky's score as raw material to be improved according to his own ideas. But the interference was also genuinely useful. The first version of Romeo and Juliet, finished in 1869 and premiered in Moscow in March 1870 under Nikolai Rubinstein, was not the masterpiece we know; it was Balakirev's continued pressure, and Tchaikovsky's own dissatisfaction, that drove the revisions. The work exists in three versions: the original of 1869, a substantial revision of 1870, and the final version of 1880 that is almost always played today. Eleven years separate the first version from the last. The love-theme you will fall for was refined across more than a decade.

The relationship with Balakirev, like so many of Tchaikovsky's relationships with the nationalists, later cooled. But the gift of Romeo and Juliet stands, and the dedication of the work to Balakirev acknowledges it. Chapter 5 takes up the larger story of Tchaikovsky and the Mighty Five; here, note only that the first mature Tchaikovsky masterpiece was midwifed by the leader of the very circle to which Tchaikovsky never fully belonged.

### 4.3 Before You Listen: Romeo and Juliet, Fantasy-Overture

#### Hook Anecdote

Imagine the problem Tchaikovsky was handed. Shakespeare's _Romeo and Juliet_ is a five-act play with dozens of characters, a feud, a love affair, a secret marriage, a duel, a banishment, a fake death, a real double suicide. How do you put that into twenty minutes of orchestral music with no words, no singers, no stage? You cannot tell the plot. So you do not try. Instead you choose three things, the things music can do, and you build the whole work from them: the grave, churchy figure of Friar Laurence, who tries to help and fails; the violent feud between the two families; and the love of Romeo and Juliet themselves. Three musical ideas, set against each other, fought to a tragic conclusion.

That is the genius of the form Tchaikovsky chose, the "fantasy-overture," a one-movement orchestral piece that captures the essence of a drama without narrating it. He does not show you the balcony scene or the duel as events. He gives you the feeling of feud, the feeling of love, the presence of the helpless wise friar, and he lets them collide. By the end, the love-theme, which sang so ardently in the middle, returns broken and grieving over a funereal drumbeat, and you understand, without a single word, that the lovers are dead. It is one of the most economical pieces of dramatic storytelling in all of music.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The solemn opening: Friar Laurence.** The work begins slowly and quietly, with woodwinds (clarinets and bassoons) playing a grave, hymn-like, churchy theme. This is Friar Laurence, the monk who marries the lovers in secret and whose plan goes tragically wrong. Listen for the chorale-like solemnity; it is the sound of the church, of wise counsel, of fate looking on. This music will return at a crucial moment.

  2. **The feud: violent, fast, clashing.** After the slow introduction, the music erupts into a fast, agitated, aggressive section. This is the feud between the Montagues and Capulets, the street-fighting of the play's first scene. Listen for the sharp, jabbing rhythms, the cymbal clashes, the sense of swords and running feet. The music is genuinely violent, and it is the engine of the whole conflict.

  3. **The love-theme.** Out of the violence, a famous melody emerges, one of the great love-tunes in music. It is introduced quietly, first in the warm middle register (the English horn and muted violas), then taken up and expanded by the strings into a soaring, ardent statement. Listen for the moment the fighting falls away and this long, yearning melody rises. When it returns, transformed and grieving, at the very end, you will know the story has ended in death.

#### One-sentence background

The Romeo and Juliet Fantasy-Overture, after Shakespeare, is a one-movement orchestral work composed in 1869 and revised in 1870 and again in 1880 (the final version is the one normally played); it was suggested and closely guided by Mily Balakirev, to whom it is dedicated; it is in B minor, ends in B major, runs about twenty minutes, and the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Herbert von Karajan conducting the Berlin Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon (Karajan recorded the work more than once; any of his Berlin accounts from the 1960s onward applies), chosen for its weight, its sweep in the love-theme, and its standing as a reference performance.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Tchaikovsky Romeo and Juliet Fantasy Overture Karajan Berlin" or "Tchaikovsky Romeo and Juliet Overture" ZH: "柴可夫斯基 罗密欧与朱丽叶 幻想序曲 卡拉扬 柏林" 或 "柴可夫斯基 罗密欧与朱丽叶" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Leonard Bernstein with the New York Philharmonic on Sony (1950s-60s), an intense, theatrical reading that leans into the drama.
* Riccardo Muti with the Philharmonia Orchestra (1980s), a clean, propulsive account; or Valery Gergiev with a leading orchestra for a darker, more Russian sound.

### 4.4 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Karajan / Berlin recording, in which the work runs about twenty minutes. The structural moments below hold for any recording; the clock times will differ.

**0:00. Friar Laurence: the chorale.** The work opens slowly and softly. Clarinets and bassoons play the grave, hymn-like Friar Laurence theme. The mood is churchy, solemn, a little foreboding. Listen for the chorale character: it is the sound of religious counsel and of fate.

**About 1:40. The introduction darkens and tightens.** The slow introduction develops; harp arpeggios and string figures add color and tension. Listen for a sense of gathering, of the calm before the storm. The music is building toward the eruption of the feud.

**About 4:40. The feud erupts.** The Allegro begins. The music breaks into fast, violent, syncopated fighting music, with stabbing string figures and cymbal crashes. This is the street-brawl of the Montagues and Capulets. Feel the aggression and the speed.

**About 6:00. The love-theme appears.** Out of the violence, the famous love-theme enters, first quietly, in the English horn and muted violas, a warm, yearning melody in the middle of the orchestra. A second, gentler idea (often described as the lovers' tenderness, in muted strings) answers it. Listen for the complete change of world: the fighting is gone, and we are in the lovers' private warmth.

**About 8:00. The love-theme soars.** The strings take up the love-theme and expand it into a full, soaring, passionate statement. This is the emotional peak of the work's first half, the love at its most ardent and most hopeful. Let it carry you; Tchaikovsky means it to.

**About 10:00. The development: feud and friar return.** The fighting music comes back, now combined with the Friar Laurence chorale, which sounds out against the violence. Listen for the chorale theme cutting through the battle: the wise friar trying, and failing, to hold the conflict back. This combination of love, feud, and friar is the dramatic core of the piece.

**About 13:00. The love-theme returns in glory, then is overwhelmed.** The love-theme comes back even more grandly, a last great surge of hope. But the feud music will not be denied; the conflict overwhelms the love, building to a violent climax.

**About 16:30. The catastrophe and the funeral.** The music crashes to a stop. Then, over a slow, soft, funereal timpani beat, the love-theme returns one last time, broken, grieving, in fragments. This is the death of the lovers. Listen for the love-theme made elegiac, the same melody that soared now mourning. The contrast with its earlier ardor is the whole tragedy in sound.

**About 19:00. The final chords.** After the grief, the work ends with solemn, affirming chords in B major, the timpani, the full orchestra, a sense of transfiguration after death. The lovers are gone, but the love is somehow exalted. The work has taken about twenty minutes and has told Shakespeare's tragedy without a single word.

### 4.5 What's Happening in the Music

#### The fantasy-overture form

Romeo and Juliet is built on sonata form, the standard architecture of a symphonic first movement, which Tchaikovsky learned in his Conservatory training and which underlies most of the large works in this book. In sonata form, two contrasting themes (the "subjects") are stated, then developed and combined in a middle section, then restated. Here the two subjects are the feud (the fast, violent music) and the love (the soaring melody), and the slow Friar Laurence music frames the whole as an introduction and recurs in the development.

The brilliance is how perfectly the dramatic situation maps onto the musical form. Sonata form is built on the conflict of two themes; _Romeo and Juliet_ is built on the conflict of two forces, hate and love. The development section, where themes collide and combine, is exactly where the friar's chorale sounds against the feud. The recapitulation, where the themes return, is where the love-theme makes its last glorious stand before the feud destroys it. Tchaikovsky did not impose a foreign program on an abstract form; he found a play whose shape was already a sonata. This is what "achieved" means here, and what "Winter Dreams" had not yet reached: the structure and the meaning are the same thing.

One concrete detail shows how exactly the structure carries the meaning, and it is something you can hear without reading a note. The love-theme first sings in D-flat major, warm and settled. When it returns in the recapitulation, Tchaikovsky brings it back a half-step higher, in D major, fortissimo across the full strings. That single step up, and the sudden blaze of the whole orchestra, make the return sound less like a homecoming than like a desperate over-reaching, the love straining for a height it cannot quite hold. And it is precisely at this over-bright peak that the feud music crashes back to destroy it. The "rigor underneath the melody" that this book keeps promising is audible right here: the placement and the key of a tune's return are doing the dramatic work that, in a play, dialogue and action would do.

For Thread B of this volume, register Romeo and Juliet as the first full example of Tchaikovsky's programmatic-narrative mastery, the ability to make a symphonic structure carry a dramatic story. Chapter 18 §18.2 collects the programmatic works (Romeo and Juliet, the _1812 Overture_ , _Francesca da Rimini_ , _Hamlet_ , the _Manfred Symphony_) and shows how the technique developed from this root.

#### The love-theme as melodic invention

The love-theme is the chapter's other claim on Thread B, the melodic-invention thread launched in Chapter 2. It is the supreme example, this early, of the long arching Tchaikovsky melody: a wide, yearning line that rises by steps and leaps toward a peak and then falls, sustained over rich harmony, scored to glow. It is the kind of tune that lodges in the memory on a single hearing, and it has been borrowed, quoted, and parodied so often that it can be hard to hear freshly. Try to hear it as the lovers' music, tender at its first appearance, ardent at its soaring statement, broken at its grieving return.

What makes it a great melody and not merely a pretty one is what Tchaikovsky does with it across the work. A lesser composer would state a love-theme and leave it. Tchaikovsky transforms it: the same notes that sing of hope at 8:00 mourn over a funeral drum at 16:30. The melody carries the whole emotional journey of the drama, from love to death, by changing its harmony, its scoring, its dynamic, while keeping its identity. This is melodic invention plus the treatment of melody, the calibration named in Chapter 2, working at the highest level. Chapter 18 §18.1 collects the great Tchaikovsky melodies; the love-theme is among the first the reader should remember.

#### The orchestral color

Notice, throughout, the specificity of the scoring. Friar Laurence is woodwinds, clarinets and bassoons, for a churchy, reedy solemnity. The love-theme is introduced by the English horn (a low, dark, expressive woodwind) and muted violas, for warmth and intimacy, before the full strings take it up for the soaring statement. The feud is strings and cymbals and brass, for violence. The death is the timpani's soft funeral beat under the broken melody. Tchaikovsky chooses each color to characterize each idea, and the choices are so apt that you can almost follow the drama by instrument alone. This precision of orchestral characterization is one of his signatures, and it reaches its fullest flowering in the ballets (Chapters 7, 15, 16), where every character and scene gets its own instrumental color. The forward pointer to the orchestral-color thread, collected in Chapter 18 §18.3, is here.

### 4.6 The Stories

#### The three versions

The eleven-year revision history of Romeo and Juliet is worth dwelling on, because it overturns the fantasy of the inspired Romantic who pours out a finished masterpiece in a fever. Tchaikovsky finished a complete version in 1869. He was not satisfied; neither was Balakirev, who kept up his stream of detailed criticism. Tchaikovsky revised the work substantially in 1870, changing the introduction and other passages. And then, a full decade later, in 1880, he revised it again, arriving at the version we play today, with its final coda. The most famous twenty minutes of his early maturity took eleven years to reach their final form.

The lesson is the one from Chapter 4's opening: Tchaikovsky was a reviser and a worker, not a one-take genius. The fluency of the melodies conceals the labor of the structure. Knowing that the love-theme's setting was refined across eleven years does not diminish it; it deepens it, the way knowing that a poem was drafted many times deepens a reader's respect for the final line. When you hear the perfect placement of the love-theme's grieving return at the end, remember that Tchaikovsky tried other endings and rejected them. The rightness is earned.

#### Balakirev's letters

The correspondence between Balakirev and Tchaikovsky over Romeo and Juliet is one of the revealing documents of 19th-century music, because it shows one composer trying to direct another's imagination in detail. Balakirev suggested the key scheme, proposed thematic ideas, and criticized what Tchaikovsky produced, sometimes harshly. He wanted the Friar Laurence music to sound a particular way and made Tchaikovsky rewrite it. He had strong views about the love-music. Tchaikovsky, younger and less sure of himself, took much of the advice, resisted some of it, and produced something better than either of them might have alone.

There is a question worth sitting with: how much of Romeo and Juliet is Tchaikovsky and how much is Balakirev? The honest answer is that the themes and their treatment are Tchaikovsky's, unmistakably so; no one else wrote melodies like the love-theme. But the plan, the discipline, the pressure to revise, much of that is Balakirev's. The work is a collaboration in the loose sense that a great editor collaborates with an author: the words are the author's, but the shape owes something to the editor's insistence. As Tchaikovsky grew in confidence, he needed this kind of direction less and resented it more, and the relationship with Balakirev and the nationalists cooled accordingly. But for this one work, at this one moment, the older man's pressure was a gift.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

Romeo and Juliet connects forward across the book.

To the programmatic orchestral works. The _1812 Overture_ (Chapter 13) and _Francesca da Rimini_ (mentioned in Chapter 13 and collected in Chapter 18) are later examples of the same fantasy-overture instinct, telling or evoking a story in one orchestral movement. So, on the largest scale, is the _Manfred Symphony_ (Chapter 13). Romeo and Juliet is the model from which they descend.

To the love-music tradition. The soaring love-theme here is the ancestor of Tchaikovsky's other great love-music: the white-swan adagio of _Swan Lake_ (Chapter 7), the Tatiana Letter Scene of _Eugene Onegin_ (Chapter 10), the love-duets of the operas and ballets. Chapter 18 §18.1 collects them.

To the Balakirev relationship and the Mighty Five. The next chapter, Chapter 5, takes up in full the story of Tchaikovsky and the nationalist circle of which Balakirev was the leader. Romeo and Juliet is the single most important fruit of Tchaikovsky's contact with that circle, and it stands as evidence that the relationship, for all its friction, was musically productive.

#### Why this is the first mature masterwork

It is worth being precise about why Romeo and Juliet, and not the First Symphony, marks the arrival of the mature Tchaikovsky. The First Symphony has beautiful melodies and a clear program, but its structure is uneven; the development sections wander, the architecture creaks. Romeo and Juliet has melodies just as beautiful and a structure that is flawless, because Tchaikovsky found a subject whose dramatic shape was identical to the musical form he was building. The love-theme and the feud are not decorations on a sonata; they are the sonata. The friar's chorale is not an introduction tacked on; it is the frame that gives the conflict its meaning. Everything is integrated, and integration, the fusion of melody and structure and meaning, is what separates a masterpiece from a promising piece. From 1870 onward, with this work, Tchaikovsky is a master. The rest of the book follows the master at his work.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Tchaikovsky represents Shakespeare's whole tragedy with just three musical ideas: Friar Laurence, the feud, and the love. Listen for each of the three when it first appears. Why these three? What can these three ideas, set against each other, tell you about the play that a literal retelling of the plot could not?"_
* _"The love-theme appears three times: tender (around 6:00), soaring (around 8:00), and broken over a funeral drum (around 16:30). Listen to all three. The notes are nearly the same; the meaning changes completely. How does Tchaikovsky transform the same melody from hope to grief, and what does this tell you about his idea of what a melody can do?"_
* _"In the development, the Friar Laurence chorale sounds out against the violent feud music. Listen for that combination. Dramatically, what is happening? Why does Tchaikovsky set the wise friar's theme against the fighting rather than against the love?"_
* _"This masterpiece took eleven years and three versions to reach its final form, much of it under Mily Balakirev's detailed and sometimes overbearing direction. Does knowing that the work was so heavily revised and guided change how you hear its 'inspired' love-theme? What does the long revision history tell you about how Tchaikovsky actually worked?"_
* _"The work is in B minor but ends in B major, with solemn, exalted chords after the lovers' death. Listen to the final minute. Is this a happy ending, a tragic one, or something else, a transfiguration? What does Tchaikovsky want you to feel about the lovers as the music closes?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Karajan / Berlin recording (or one of the alternatives in §4.3) and listen again, attending to these moments:

  1. **Friar Laurence's chorale (0:00).** Confirm the grave, hymn-like woodwind theme. Register its churchy solemnity; this is the music of counsel and fate, and it will return.

  2. **The feud erupts (around 4:40).** Listen for the fast, violent, syncopated fighting music with its cymbal crashes. Feel the aggression; this is the engine of the tragedy.

  3. **The love-theme soars (around 8:00).** Listen for the strings expanding the love-theme into its full ardent statement. This is love at its most hopeful, the peak of the work's first half.

  4. **The grieving return over the funeral drum (around 16:30).** The chapter's emotional climax. Listen for the broken love-theme over the soft timpani beat, the same melody that soared now mourning. This is the death of the lovers, told without a word.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : hear Romeo and Juliet and then, if you can find it, a few minutes of one of the other versions of the love-theme's setting, or simply listen to the love-theme three times in a row at its three appearances (tender, soaring, grieving). The exercise takes fifteen minutes and trains your ear to track a single melody through transformation, which is the central Tchaikovsky listening skill and the thing Chapter 18 will ask of you across the whole catalogue.

**Read** : the chapter on Romeo and Juliet and the Balakirev relationship in David Brown's _Tchaikovsky: A Biographical and Critical Study_ (the early-years volume), which discusses the three versions and quotes the Balakirev correspondence, or the corresponding material in Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (2009). Brown is especially good on the technical side of the revisions and on what Balakirev contributed; reading him will prepare you for Chapter 5, where the larger story of Tchaikovsky and the Mighty Five unfolds.

---

## Chapter 5: The Mighty Five versus Tchaikovsky

To understand Tchaikovsky, you have to understand what he was not. In the Russia of his maturity, the dominant idea of what Russian music should be belonged to a group of composers in St. Petersburg who held almost the opposite of Tchaikovsky's convictions. They believed in a native Russian art music built from Russian folk material and Russian subjects, made by composers who learned their craft outside the academy, free of the German conservatory tradition. Tchaikovsky was the great Russian composer who did not belong to them, who was trained in exactly the academy they distrusted, who revered Mozart and built Western symphonies, and who used Russian folk material in a thoroughly un-nationalist way. The friction between Tchaikovsky and this group is one of the defining facts of his career, and it is the key to hearing what is specifically his in the music.

This chapter has no single anchor work. Instead it asks you to listen comparatively: to sample the sound of the nationalist composers, the Mighty Five, and to set it against the sound of Tchaikovsky, so that you can hear the difference for yourself. The difference is not that one side is Russian and the other is not; both are deeply Russian. The difference is in how each side thought a Russian composer should work, and the sound of that difference is something your ear can learn to catch.

This chapter also establishes a principle, §5.T2 in this book's editorial frame, that runs through the whole volume: the honest account of Tchaikovsky's Russian-Western position. He was neither a nationalist nor a Western imitator. He was a hybrid, and the hybrid was a deliberate, considered stance, not a failure to be one thing or the other. Chapter 20 synthesizes the whole question; here we lay the ground.

### 5.1 Who were the Mighty Five?

In 1867 the influential St. Petersburg critic Vladimir Stasov wrote a newspaper notice praising a concert and referred to a "mighty little heap" of Russian composers, the _moguchaya kuchka_ , the "mighty handful." The phrase stuck, and the group it named became known in English as the Mighty Five, or simply the Five. The members were Mily Balakirev, the leader, whom we met in Chapter 4 as the guiding hand behind Romeo and Juliet; César Cui; Modest Mussorgsky; Nikolai Rimsky-Korsakov; and Alexander Borodin.

Several facts about the Five define them. First, almost none of them was a trained professional musician by the standards of the conservatory. Borodin was a distinguished research chemist who composed in his spare time. Mussorgsky had been an army officer and then a low-level civil servant. Rimsky-Korsakov was a naval officer who first went to sea and only later became a professional musician. Cui was a military engineer, an expert on fortifications, who composed and wrote criticism on the side. Only Balakirev was a full-time musician, and even he was largely self-taught. This was, in part, deliberate. The Five regarded the conservatory training, with its German textbooks and its rules of harmony and counterpoint, as a foreign imposition that produced derivative, academic music. They believed a truly Russian music would come from Russian instinct and Russian materials, not from German rulebooks.

Second, their program was nationalist. They wanted music built on Russian folk song, Russian church chant, Russian subjects, Russian history and legend. Mussorgsky's _Boris Godunov_ is an opera about a Russian tsar, built on Russian declamation and Russian color. Borodin's _Prince Igor_ draws on a medieval Russian epic. Rimsky-Korsakov filled his scores with Russian and Eastern folk-flavored material. They looked to Mikhail Glinka, the founding father of Russian art music, as their ancestor, and they aimed to build a national school on his foundation.

Third, they had a critic-champion in Stasov, who promoted their cause in the press, and an opponent's gallery that included the conservatory faction, the Rubinstein brothers, and, in their eyes, Tchaikovsky. The musical politics of 1860s and 1870s Russia were fierce, conducted in newspapers and salons, and a composer's allegiance mattered.

### 5.2 Where Tchaikovsky stood

Tchaikovsky stood almost exactly opposite the Five on the central questions, while sharing their Russianness and, often, their company.

He was conservatory-trained, the product of the very institution the Five distrusted, and he believed in that training. He thought their amateurism, however gifted, led to technical weakness, to music that was vivid in invention but clumsy in construction. He once wrote, in private letters, sharply critical assessments of the Five's craftsmanship even while admiring their talent: that Mussorgsky's gifts were real but undisciplined; that Borodin's technique was limited by his lack of training; that Cui was a dilettante. He admired Rimsky-Korsakov most, partly because Rimsky-Korsakov, alone among the Five, eventually submitted to systematic study and became a conservatory professor himself, mastering the craft the others scorned.

He was cosmopolitan where they were nationalist. His gods were Mozart above all, then the Italian opera composers, the French, the German symphonic line. He built sonata-form symphonies and concertos on Western models. When he used Russian folk song, and he used it often, he set it inside Western structures, harmonized it with Western harmony, developed it with Western technique. He did not believe a Russian composer should reject the European inheritance; he believed a Russian composer should master it and bring a Russian sensibility to it.

And yet he was as Russian as any of them. The melancholy, the breadth, the lyricism, the folk-song coloring of his music are unmistakably Russian. He loved Russian folk song genuinely, collected it, used it throughout his work. He was not a Westerner in Russian dress; he was a Russian who had absorbed the West. The difference between him and the Five is not Russian versus non-Russian. It is two different ideas of how a Russian composer should relate to the Western tradition: reject and replace it (the Five), or master and inflect it (Tchaikovsky).

### 5.3 The friction, and the respect

The relationship was tense but not simply hostile. The sharpest edge was César Cui, who as a critic wrote hostile reviews of Tchaikovsky's works, and Vladimir Stasov, whose nationalist polemics had no place for Tchaikovsky's cosmopolitanism. Tchaikovsky felt the hostility and resented it, and his private letters about the Five can be cutting. There was real wounded feeling on his side, the feeling of an outsider looking at a club that would not fully admit him.

But there was also genuine contact and mutual respect. Balakirev guided Romeo and Juliet and, later, suggested the _Manfred Symphony_. Tchaikovsky became friendly with Rimsky-Korsakov and praised his gifts. When Tchaikovsky's Second Symphony, the "Little Russian," was new, he played its folk-based finale at a gathering at Rimsky-Korsakov's home, and the Five were delighted by it, because in that one work Tchaikovsky had come closest to their own folk-nationalist manner. The relationship was the complicated, competitive, sometimes warm relationship of rival artists who shared a country and a moment, not the clean enmity that a tidy story would prefer. As with the Tolstoy anecdote in Chapter 2 and the Balakirev collaboration in Chapter 4, the truth is messier and more human than the legend.

### 5.4 Listening comparatively

The best way to hear the difference is to listen. Sample the nationalist sound, then return to a Tchaikovsky touchstone you already know, and let your ear find the contrast. None of these is a full anchor work for this book; treat them as comparative listening, twenty minutes of sampling rather than a deep dive.

#### Mussorgsky, _Pictures at an Exhibition_ (1874)

Mussorgsky wrote _Pictures at an Exhibition_ as a suite of piano pieces depicting paintings by a friend who had died; it is most often heard today in the orchestration Maurice Ravel made in 1922. Listen to a few of its movements: the recurring "Promenade" theme, the "Great Gate of Kiev" at the end. What you hear is vivid, original, harmonically unconventional music, built from blunt, strong, often modal materials, with a roughness and directness that is part of its power. Mussorgsky's harmony breaks the textbook rules, sometimes by ignorance, sometimes by instinct, and the result is a sound no conservatory would have produced. This is the nationalist instinct at its most powerful: raw, Russian, original, and technically irregular. A reliable recording is Fritz Reiner with the Chicago Symphony (RCA, 1957, in the Ravel orchestration) or any of the many well-reviewed accounts.

#### Borodin, Polovtsian Dances from _Prince Igor_

Borodin's opera _Prince Igor_ was left unfinished at his death and completed by Rimsky-Korsakov and Alexander Glazunov; its most famous excerpt is the Polovtsian Dances, a set of exotic, folk-flavored dances. Listen for the sinuous, "Eastern" melodies (one of them became the song "Stranger in Paradise" in a 20th-century musical), the driving rhythms, the rich orchestral color. Borodin, the chemist-composer, had a natural gift for melody and color and a limited command of large-scale construction; the Polovtsian Dances are a string of marvelous tunes and effects rather than a tightly argued structure, and they are glorious. A well-reviewed recording in any major _Prince Igor_ set or orchestral compilation applies.

#### Rimsky-Korsakov, _Scheherazade_ (1888)

Rimsky-Korsakov's _Scheherazade_ , a symphonic suite on tales from the _Thousand and One Nights_ , is the nationalist school's masterpiece of orchestral color, written by the one member of the Five who mastered conservatory technique. Listen for the dazzling orchestration, the recurring solo-violin theme representing the storyteller Scheherazade, the depiction of the sea and the ships and the Eastern court. Here the nationalist-exotic instinct is married to real technical command, and the result is one of the most brilliantly scored works in the repertoire. A classic recording is Thomas Beecham with the Royal Philharmonic (EMI, 1957) or Kirill Kondrashin with a leading orchestra; both are well-reviewed and decades old.

#### Back to Tchaikovsky

Now return to the Romeo and Juliet Fantasy-Overture from Chapter 4, or the slow movement of "Winter Dreams" from Chapter 3. Listen for what is different. The Tchaikovsky melodies are longer, more arching, more Italian-operatic in their breadth than the short, modal, folk-cell melodies of Mussorgsky. The harmony is richer and more orthodox, schooled rather than rule-breaking. The structure is tighter, more thoroughly developed, more Western-symphonic. And the orchestration, while as colorful as Rimsky-Korsakov's, serves a dramatic argument rather than a tableau. The Russianness is still there, in the melancholy and the lyricism, but it is filtered through a Western training that the Five rejected.

The contrast is not a matter of better and worse. Mussorgsky's roughness is a glory; Tchaikovsky's polish is a glory; they are different glories. But once you have heard the difference, you can hear, in any Tchaikovsky work, the specific thing he was: a Russian who chose the West and brought Russia to it.

### 5.5 The Stories

#### The Glinka inheritance, claimed by both

Both Tchaikovsky and the Five revered Mikhail Glinka (1804-1857), the founding father of Russian art music, composer of the operas _A Life for the Tsar_ and _Ruslan and Lyudmila_. But they claimed different parts of him. The Five claimed Glinka the nationalist, the user of Russian folk material and Russian subjects. Tchaikovsky claimed Glinka the orchestral colorist and melodist, and especially the Glinka of the orchestral fantasy _Kamarinskaya_ , which Tchaikovsky famously said contained the whole of Russian symphonic music "as the oak is in the acorn." That a single ancestor could be claimed by both camps tells you that the division was not about whether to be Russian but about how. Glinka had been both a nationalist and a cosmopolitan, both a folk-user and a Western-trained colorist, and his heirs divided his legacy between them.

#### The cost to Tchaikovsky of not belonging

It is worth feeling the human cost of Tchaikovsky's position, because it shaped his temperament. He was, in the musical politics of his own country, an outsider: too Western for the nationalists, too Russian and too emotional for some of the academic conservatives, attacked in the press by Cui, never fully at home in either camp. This outsider status compounded the loneliness and insecurity that were already part of his nature. He craved approval and was wounded by hostile criticism to a degree that strikes modern readers as excessive; a single bad review could devastate him for days. Some of that sensitivity was temperamental, but some of it was the real situation of a man who had no faction to defend him, who stood alone between the parties.

The compensation, in the end, was that standing alone, he became the most universally beloved of all Russian composers, the one whose music traveled furthest beyond Russia precisely because it was not narrowly nationalist. The Five's nationalism made them powerful at home and slower to travel; Tchaikovsky's hybrid art made him the Russian composer the whole world adopted. The outsider position that cost him so much in his lifetime was the source of his global reach. Chapter 20 develops this fully.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Russian-Western question runs through the whole book.

To the Second Symphony, "Little Russian" (Chapter 6), the work in which Tchaikovsky came closest to the Five's folk-nationalist manner and won their praise. It is the best evidence that the difference between him and them was a matter of degree and emphasis, not an absolute wall.

To the folk-song integration thread. The way Tchaikovsky uses Russian and Ukrainian folk song, heard at chamber scale in Chapter 2 and orchestral scale in Chapter 3, is the practical site of the Russian-Western question. Every time he quotes a folk tune (the Second Symphony, the finale of the Fourth, the First Piano Concerto), he is doing the thing the Five also did, but doing it his way, inside Western structures.

To Chapter 20, the synthesis. There the whole question, Tchaikovsky's relation to the Five and to the West, and his legacy to Rachmaninoff and Stravinsky and Shostakovich, is gathered and resolved. Carry it with you: keep the Russian-Western tension in mind through every chapter, and Chapter 20 will collect what you have heard.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Both Tchaikovsky and the Mighty Five were deeply Russian; what divided them was how a Russian composer should relate to the Western tradition. Listen to a few minutes of Mussorgsky's _Pictures at an Exhibition_ and then to Tchaikovsky's Romeo and Juliet. Can you hear the difference in how each treats melody, harmony, and structure? Describe it in your own words."_
* _"Mussorgsky's harmony 'breaks the rules,' sometimes by instinct, sometimes by lack of training, and the roughness is part of its power. Tchaikovsky's harmony is schooled and orthodox. Is one approach more 'Russian' than the other? Is technical correctness a virtue or a limitation, and does your answer change depending on the piece?"_
* _"Rimsky-Korsakov was the one member of the Five who eventually mastered conservatory technique, and _Scheherazade_ shows nationalist color married to real craft. Listen to a few minutes of it. Does the technical command make it more like Tchaikovsky, less like the other four, or does it remain distinctly nationalist? What survives of the kuchka instinct even when the craft is conservatory-trained?"_
* _"Tchaikovsky was an outsider in his own country's musical politics, too Western for the nationalists, attacked in the press, belonging to no faction. The chapter argues this outsider status was both a wound and the secret of his global reach. How might not belonging to a camp have freed his music to travel? Can you think of other artists whose lack of a 'school' helped them reach a wider audience?"_
* _"Both camps claimed Glinka as their ancestor, the Five claiming the nationalist Glinka and Tchaikovsky claiming the colorist-melodist Glinka. What does it tell you about a founding figure that rival movements can each claim part of him? If you had to divide Tchaikovsky's own legacy between a 'nationalist' and a 'cosmopolitan' heir, how would you split it?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is a comparative-listening chapter, so the re-listen is a side-by-side exercise. Set aside about half an hour and listen, in order, to:

  1. **A few minutes of Mussorgsky,_Pictures at an Exhibition_** (the "Promenade" and the "Great Gate of Kiev"). Register the blunt, strong, modal, rule-breaking sound of the nationalist instinct.

  2. **The Polovtsian Dances from Borodin's _Prince Igor_**. Register the gift for melody and exotic color, and the looser, tableau-like structure.

  3. **A few minutes of Rimsky-Korsakov's _Scheherazade_** (the opening, with the solo-violin Scheherazade theme). Register nationalist color joined to conservatory craft.

  4. **The love-theme of Tchaikovsky's Romeo and Juliet** (Chapter 4) once more. Register the longer, more arching, more operatic melody, the richer schooled harmony, the tighter structure. This is the Western-trained Russian, and the contrast with the three samples above is the lesson of the chapter.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : if the comparison interests you, hear a complete performance of Mussorgsky's _Pictures at an Exhibition_ in the Ravel orchestration alongside a complete Tchaikovsky orchestral work such as Romeo and Juliet. The pairing, about forty minutes, is the clearest single demonstration of the two Russian approaches, the nationalist-instinctive and the cosmopolitan-schooled, and you will come away able to hear which camp almost any later Russian piece descends from.

**Read** : the chapters on Tchaikovsky and the nationalists in David Brown's biography, or the relevant sections of Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (2009). For the other side of the story, the standard English account of the Five is in general histories of Russian music; Rimsky-Korsakov's own memoir, _My Musical Life_ , gives a vivid insider's picture of the circle and its quarrels, including its dealings with Tchaikovsky, to be read with the awareness that it is a participant's partial account. These readings will deepen Chapter 6, where Tchaikovsky meets the Five on their own folk-nationalist ground in the "Little Russian" Symphony, and Chapter 20, where the whole question is resolved.

---

## Chapter 6: Symphony No. 2 "Little Russian" and Symphony No. 3 "Polish"

Between the First Symphony of 1866 and the great Fourth of 1878 lie two symphonies that most listeners never reach: the Second, "Little Russian," and the Third, "Polish." They are not as ambitious as what came after, and they are seldom programmed. But they are the two works in which you can watch Tchaikovsky solving the problem the First Symphony left open, the problem of how to build a satisfying symphonic structure, and the Second in particular is the work in which he came closest to the nationalist manner of the Mighty Five and won, for once, their wholehearted praise. This chapter anchors on the Second and visits the Third, and it asks you to hear the runway from which the Fourth Symphony would take off.

The two nicknames need explaining at the start, because both point to nations and neither was Tchaikovsky's own. "Little Russia" was the term the 19th-century Russian Empire used for Ukraine, and the Second Symphony earned its nickname because it is built on Ukrainian folk songs. "Polish" attached to the Third because its finale is a polonaise, a Polish dance. Neither name tells you the symphony is about Poland or Ukraine in any deep sense; both are labels that critics and audiences pinned on the works for a single salient feature. We use them because everyone does, while keeping clear what they do and do not mean.

### 6.1 The "Little Russian" Symphony and the folk songs of Ukraine

Tchaikovsky composed the Second Symphony in 1872, much of it at Kamenka, the Ukrainian estate of his sister Alexandra and her husband Lev Davydov, where he spent many summers and where, three years earlier, he had noted down the folk tune that became the Andante cantabile of Chapter 2. Ukraine, "Little Russia" in the imperial usage, was the source of the folk material that gives the symphony its character. Tchaikovsky used three Ukrainian folk songs in the work, most prominently in the outer movements.

The first movement opens with a solo horn playing a Ukrainian variant of a folk melody, slow and grave, before the main Allegro begins. The finale, the most celebrated movement, is built on a Ukrainian folk song known as "The Crane," a short, catchy, dancing tune that Tchaikovsky subjects to a brilliant set of variations and developments, repeating it again and again in ever-changing orchestral guises until it builds to a blazing close. The technique is exactly what the Mighty Five admired: a real folk tune, kept recognizably folk-like, made the engine of an entire symphonic movement.

The nickname "Little Russian" was coined by the critic Nikolai Kashkin, a friend of Tchaikovsky's, and it stuck. The symphony was first performed in Moscow in early 1873 under Nikolai Rubinstein and was a real success, the warmest reception any of Tchaikovsky's symphonies had yet had. And it was the work that delighted the Mighty Five. Tchaikovsky played the finale at a gathering at Nikolai Rimsky-Korsakov's home, and the nationalist circle, who so often found him too Western, were thrilled by this folk-based, folk-spirited movement. For once, the outsider had spoken their language.

There is a complication worth noting, the kind of revision history we met with Romeo and Juliet. Tchaikovsky was never fully satisfied with the Second Symphony, and in 1879-1880 he revised it substantially, rewriting the first movement almost entirely and tightening the others. The version we normally hear is this revised one. So even this folk-spirited success was, like so much of his work, the product of reconsideration and rebuilding years after the first version.

### 6.2 Before You Listen: Symphony No. 2 "Little Russian," finale

#### Hook Anecdote

Picture the gathering at Rimsky-Korsakov's apartment in St. Petersburg, sometime after the symphony's premiere. The Mighty Five are there, the nationalist circle that has so often kept Tchaikovsky at arm's length, that has criticized him in the press as too Western, too academic, too European. And Tchaikovsky sits down and plays them the finale of his new symphony, built on the Ukrainian folk song "The Crane." And they love it. They are, by the accounts that survive, delighted, enthusiastic, won over. For one evening, the gap between Tchaikovsky and the Five closes, because in this one movement he has done the thing they most prize: taken a humble Russian folk tune and made a thrilling symphonic movement out of it, without smothering its folk character.

The moment is a small reconciliation in a long, prickly relationship, and it tells you something important. The difference between Tchaikovsky and the Five, which Chapter 5 laid out, was real but not absolute. When Tchaikovsky chose to meet them on their ground, he could do it superbly. The finale of the "Little Russian" is the proof, and it is one of the most purely enjoyable things he ever wrote.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening horn solo (first movement).** If you hear the first movement, listen for the very opening: a solo horn, alone, playing a slow, grave, folk-derived melody. It is one of the most beautiful symphony openings Tchaikovsky wrote, and it sets the Ukrainian-folk character of the whole work.

  2. **The folk tune "The Crane" in the finale.** The finale opens with grand, attention-getting chords, and then states the folk tune "The Crane," a short, bouncy, dancing melody. Listen for it, and then listen for how Tchaikovsky repeats it, over and over, dressing it in different orchestral colors and harmonies each time, building energy. The whole finale is essentially this one tune, varied and developed into a symphonic blaze.

  3. **The accumulating excitement.** The finale's method is accumulation: the folk tune returns again and again, each time more brilliantly scored, the orchestra growing fuller, the energy higher, until the close is a roar of celebration. Listen for the sense of a single idea building relentlessly toward a triumphant finish. This is folk material driving a symphonic engine, and it is irresistible.

#### One-sentence background

The Symphony No. 2 in C minor, "Little Russian," Op. 17, was composed in 1872 (and substantially revised in 1879-80, the version normally heard), built on three Ukrainian folk songs, most prominently the tune "The Crane" in the finale; it runs about thirty-five minutes, earned its nickname (from the imperial term for Ukraine) from the critic Nikolai Kashkin, and the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Mariss Jansons with the Oslo Philharmonic on Chandos (the same complete cycle as Chapter 3), chosen for its rhythmic vitality in the finale and its idiomatic folk feeling.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Tchaikovsky Symphony No. 2 Little Russian Jansons Oslo" or "Tchaikovsky Symphony 2 Op. 17 finale" ZH: "柴可夫斯基 第二交响曲 小俄罗斯 杨颂斯 奥斯陆" 或 "柴可夫斯基 第二交响曲 作品17" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Mikhail Pletnev with the Russian National Orchestra on Deutsche Grammophon (1990s), a sharply Russian, vividly characterized reading.
* Antal Doráti with the London Symphony Orchestra on Mercury (1960s), a famous vintage account with great rhythmic drive in the finale.

### 6.3 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Jansons / Oslo recording. We focus on the finale, the heart of the symphony, with a glance at the first movement's opening.

#### First movement: the opening

**0:00. The solo horn.** The symphony opens with a solo horn playing the grave, folk-derived introductory melody, alone and exposed. Register the Ukrainian-folk character and the beauty of the unaccompanied horn. This slow introduction sets the scene before the main Allegro.

#### Finale: the folk tune "The Crane"

**0:00. The grand opening chords.** The finale begins with loud, arresting orchestral chords, a fanfare to grab your attention.

**About 0:30. "The Crane" appears.** The folk tune enters, short, bouncy, dancing, stated plainly so you can catch it. Memorize its shape; you are about to hear it many times.

**About 1:15. The first variations.** The tune returns, now dressed differently, in new instrumental colors and with new accompaniment. Listen for how the melody stays recognizable while its surroundings change. This is the variation technique that drives the whole movement.

**About 2:30. A contrasting idea.** A second, more lyrical theme provides contrast, giving the ear a rest from the relentless folk tune. But "The Crane" is never far away.

**About 4:00. The accumulation intensifies.** The folk tune comes back, more brilliantly scored, the orchestra fuller, the energy rising. Tchaikovsky is building toward the close by piling up ever-richer statements of the same idea.

**About 6:30. The drive to the finish.** The variations grow more and more excited, the brass and percussion more prominent, until the music is a blaze of celebration built entirely on the little folk tune.

**About 8:00. The triumphant close.** The finale ends in a roar, the folk tune transformed into a symphonic shout of joy. The whole movement has taken a humble dance song and built a brilliant symphonic structure on it, and the effect is exhilarating.

### 6.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### Folk song as symphonic engine

The "Little Russian" finale is the clearest example in Tchaikovsky's symphonies of the technique that the Mighty Five prized and that he, in his own way, mastered: taking a folk tune and making it the structural engine of a symphonic movement. The method here is variation and repetition. A short folk tune cannot, by itself, sustain a long movement the way a developed symphonic theme can; it is too simple, too closed. So Tchaikovsky states it and then varies it relentlessly, changing the orchestration, the harmony, the accompaniment, the key, so that the same tune sounds fresh each time, and the cumulative effect of all the variations builds the structure.

This is a recognizably Russian solution, descended from Glinka's _Kamarinskaya_ , the orchestral fantasy Tchaikovsky said contained all of Russian symphonic music in embryo. _Kamarinskaya_ takes a folk tune and repeats it many times with changing accompaniment, and the "Little Russian" finale is Tchaikovsky's grandest application of the same principle. It is the most "nationalist" thing in his symphonies, the place where he is closest to the Five, which is exactly why they loved it.

For Thread B of this volume, the folk-song integration thread, register the "Little Russian" finale as the orchestral high point of Tchaikovsky's folk-tune technique. Chapter 18 §18.4 collects the folk-song uses across the catalogue, from the Andante cantabile through the Fourth Symphony's finale; the "Little Russian" is among the most important to recall when you reach that convergence.

#### Two ways of building a symphony

Set the "Little Russian" finale beside the first movement of Romeo and Juliet from Chapter 4, and you see Tchaikovsky building large structures two different ways. Romeo and Juliet uses sonata form, the Western method: two contrasting themes, developed and combined, in a dramatic argument. The "Little Russian" finale uses variation, the more Russian method: one theme, repeated and transformed, in an accumulating celebration. Tchaikovsky commanded both, and the choice between them is partly a choice between his Western and his Russian inheritances. The great mature symphonies, the Fourth especially, combine the two methods, using sonata-form drama in the first movements and folk-variation energy in the finales. The "Little Russian" is where you hear the variation method at its purest.

#### The unevenness, and the revision

The "Little Russian" is a more successful symphony than the First, but it is still not the seamless achievement the Fourth will be. The finale is a masterpiece of its kind; the inner movements are charming but slighter; and Tchaikovsky's dissatisfaction, which led to the major 1879-80 revision, was real. The work is a step on the road, not the destination. What you hear in it is a composer who has solved the problem of the folk-based finale brilliantly, and who is still working on the problem of making a whole symphony cohere as a single dramatic span. That problem he solved in 1878. The "Little Russian" is 1872, six years and one life-catastrophe short of the solution.

### 6.5 The Third Symphony, "Polish"

The Third Symphony, composed in 1875, is the odd one out among Tchaikovsky's six, and the least often played. Two things make it unusual. First, it is in five movements rather than the standard four, with an extra dance movement inserted. Second, it is the only one of his symphonies in a major key throughout, opening in a funeral-march gloom but quickly turning to a bright D major and staying broadly sunny, which gives it a lighter, more divertimento-like character than its siblings.

The nickname "Polish" comes from the finale, which is a _polonaise_ , the stately Polish processional dance (the marking is _Tempo di polacca_). It has nothing to do with Poland as a subject; an English audience simply attached the name because of the Polish dance that closes the work. The inner movements include a graceful waltz-like movement (marked _alla tedesca_ , "in the German style") and a lyrical slow movement, so that the symphony as a whole has the feel of a suite of attractive dances and songs more than a unified dramatic argument.

The Third is worth hearing once, especially the brilliant polonaise finale, which looks forward to the grand ceremonial dances of the ballets and operas (the Act III Polonaise of _Eugene Onegin_ , Chapter 10, is a close cousin). But it is fair to say that the Third is a holiday symphony, a relaxation between the folk-driven Second and the tragic Fourth, and that Tchaikovsky's deepest symphonic ambition was not yet engaged here. If you sample one movement, make it the polonaise finale, and listen for the ceremonial Tchaikovsky, the composer of grand public dances, who will dominate the ballet chapters later in the book.

### 6.6 The Stories

#### The runway to the Fourth

The reason to spend an evening with these two neglected symphonies is to feel the distance Tchaikovsky traveled to reach the Fourth. The First, Second, and Third Symphonies are all attractive, all full of good tunes, all the work of a real symphonist learning his trade. But none of them is a tragedy, none of them carries a personal drama, none of them makes the symphony into a vehicle for the deepest feeling. They are landscapes and folk celebrations and dance suites. Then, in 1877-78, after the marriage catastrophe and the beginning of the von Meck patronage, Tchaikovsky wrote the Fourth Symphony, and the symphony became, in his hands, a drama of Fate and the human struggle against it. The three early symphonies are the runway; the Fourth is the takeoff.

It is no accident that the leap coincided with the great crisis of his life. The early symphonies were written by a man whose life, however anxious, was outwardly settled: a professor, a working musician, a bachelor of regular habits. The Fourth was written by a man who had just been through a marriage that nearly destroyed him and had found a patron who set him free. The depth of the Fourth is, in part, the depth that the crisis opened in him. We turn to that crisis in Chapter 8. But hold the early symphonies in your ear, because the contrast with the Fourth is one of the most dramatic in any composer's development, and you cannot feel it unless you know what came before.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

To the Fourth Symphony (Chapters 9 and 11). The folk-variation finale of the Fourth, built on the Russian song "In the Field Stood a Birch Tree," is the direct descendant of the "Little Russian" finale's folk-variation method, now set inside a tragic symphonic drama. When you reach it, recall "The Crane."

To the ceremonial dances. The polonaise finale of the Third Symphony looks forward to the grand public dances of the operas and ballets, above all the Act III Polonaise of _Eugene Onegin_ (Chapter 10). Tchaikovsky loved the stately processional dance, and the "Polish" Symphony is an early gathering of that love.

To the folk-song thread, collected in Chapter 18 §18.4. The Ukrainian tunes of the "Little Russian" are central to the story of how Tchaikovsky used folk material, the practical site of the Russian-Western question from Chapter 5.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The finale of the 'Little Russian' Symphony is built almost entirely on one short folk tune, 'The Crane,' repeated and varied many times. Listen to the finale and count, roughly, how many times the tune returns and how it is dressed differently each time. Why does this repetition not become boring? What keeps it fresh and building rather than monotonous?"_
* _"This was the one work that won over the Mighty Five, who usually found Tchaikovsky too Western. Having heard the nationalist composers in Chapter 5, listen to the 'Little Russian' finale and identify what about it speaks the Five's language. Where do you still hear the Western-trained Tchaikovsky underneath the folk celebration?"_
* _"The chapter contrasts two ways of building a symphonic movement: sonata-form drama (two themes in conflict, as in Romeo and Juliet) and folk variation (one theme transformed many times, as in the 'Little Russian' finale). Listen to both. Which feels more 'Russian' to you, and which more 'Western'? Can you hear why the mature Fourth Symphony might want to combine them?"_
* _"The Third Symphony is described as a 'holiday symphony,' lighter and sunnier than its siblings, a relaxation between the folk-driven Second and the tragic Fourth. Listen to its polonaise finale. Is there a place in a great composer's output for music that is simply attractive and ceremonial rather than deep? What does Tchaikovsky gain, or lose, by writing a symphony that does not aim for tragedy?"_
* _"These three early symphonies are the 'runway' to the Fourth, and the leap to the Fourth coincided with the great crisis of Tchaikovsky's life in 1877. Do you think the depth of the later music required the suffering, or is that a romantic myth? Listen to the cheerful 'Little Russian' finale and ask whether a happy man could have written the tragic Fourth."_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Jansons / Oslo recording (or an alternative from §6.2) and listen again, attending to these moments:

  1. **The solo horn opening (first movement, 0:00).** Confirm the grave, exposed, folk-derived horn melody. It is one of Tchaikovsky's loveliest symphony openings.

  2. **"The Crane" stated plainly (finale, around 0:30).** Memorize the short, bouncy folk tune so you can track it through the movement.

  3. **The accumulating variations (finale, around 4:00 onward).** Listen for the same tune returning in ever-richer orchestral dress, the energy building relentlessly toward the close. This is folk-variation technique as a symphonic engine.

  4. **The polonaise finale of the Third Symphony.** If you sample the "Polish," make it this movement. Listen for the stately ceremonial dance, the public, processional Tchaikovsky who will fill the ballets and operas.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the "Little Russian" finale with Glinka's orchestral fantasy _Kamarinskaya_ , the work Tchaikovsky said contained all of Russian symphonic music in embryo. _Kamarinskaya_ uses the same folk-variation method, a folk tune repeated with changing accompaniment, and hearing it next to the "Little Russian" finale, about fifteen minutes total, shows you exactly the Russian tradition Tchaikovsky was working in when he met the Five on their own ground.

**Read** : the chapters on the early symphonies in David Brown's biography or Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (2009). Both discuss the folk sources of the Second Symphony, the revision history, and the place of these works on the road to the Fourth. Reading them will sharpen your sense of how much the catastrophe of 1877, the subject of Chapter 8, changed what Tchaikovsky could do with the symphony.
