## Chapter 13: Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 35, and the Middle-Period Years

A great violinist looked at Tchaikovsky's new violin concerto and pronounced it unplayable. A leading critic, hearing it at last, wrote that it was music that stank to the ear. And today it is one of the four or five most-played, most-loved violin concertos in the world, a fixture of every violinist's repertoire and every concert season. The Violin Concerto's path from rejection to glory is the twin of the First Piano Concerto's story from Chapter 12, and the two together teach the same lesson: that even Tchaikovsky's most immediately appealing works were, in their day, found difficult and strange, and that first reactions, even from great musicians, are a poor guide to lasting worth.

This chapter anchors on the Violin Concerto, written in Switzerland in 1878 in the long convalescence after the marriage catastrophe, and then surveys the rich band of middle-period works that followed it across the early 1880s: the _1812 Overture_ , the Serenade for Strings, the Piano Trio written in memory of Nikolai Rubinstein, the _Capriccio Italien_ , and the _Manfred Symphony_. These are the works of Tchaikovsky's settled, productive freelance decade, freed by von Meck's patronage, and they show the full range of his art, from the noisiest crowd-pleaser to the most heartfelt elegy.

### 13.1 The Violin Concerto: composition and rejection

Tchaikovsky composed the Violin Concerto in March 1878 at Clarens, on Lake Geneva in Switzerland, during the year abroad that von Meck's money made possible after the marriage crisis. He was helped by the young violinist Iosif Kotek, who advised on the violin writing and played the work through with him, and the concerto poured out of him quickly, in a burst of the renewed creative energy that the recovery brought.

He intended to dedicate it to Leopold Auer, the leading violin professor in St. Petersburg and the most influential violin pedagogue in Russia. But Auer, examining the score, declared it unplayable, technically awkward and ungrateful for the instrument, and declined to premiere it. The concerto sat unperformed for some time. It was finally premiered, not in Russia, but in Vienna, in December 1881, by the violinist Adolph Brodsky, under the conductor Hans Richter, and Tchaikovsky rededicated it to Brodsky in gratitude.

The Vienna premiere produced one of the most notorious reviews in musical history. Eduard Hanslick, the most powerful critic in Vienna and the great champion of Brahms and opponent of the "music of the future," savaged the concerto. In a phrase that has never been forgotten, he wrote that the concerto brought him face to face for the first time with the dreadful idea that there could be music that stinks to the ear. The review wounded Tchaikovsky deeply; he is said to have been able to recite it by heart years later. And like Auer's verdict, it was spectacularly wrong about the work's future. Auer himself later relented, taught the concerto to his students, and through his great pupils, it became a cornerstone of the violin repertoire. The unplayable, ear-offending concerto became one of the best-loved works ever written for the instrument.

### 13.2 Before You Listen: the Violin Concerto

#### Hook Anecdote

There is a pattern in Tchaikovsky's life that the Violin Concerto completes. The First Piano Concerto was rejected by the pianist it was meant for and triumphed elsewhere (Chapter 12). The Violin Concerto was rejected by the violinist it was meant for, savaged by the greatest critic of the age, and triumphed everywhere. _Swan Lake_ failed at its premiere and became immortal after the composer's death (Chapter 7). Again and again, the works we now think most obviously appealing were, at first, found difficult, unplayable, even offensive by the experts of the day.

This should change how you listen, and how you trust your own ear. The Violin Concerto sounds, today, like one of the most natural and grateful works ever written for the violin; it is hard to imagine anyone calling it unplayable or ear-offending. But that is hindsight; Auer and Hanslick were not fools, and the concerto genuinely was new and difficult in 1878-81, asking things of violinists and listeners they had not been asked before. What sounds inevitable now was strange then. When you hear the soaring main theme and the brilliant finale, remember that this music had to fight its way into the repertoire against the best-informed opinion of its time, and let that make you humble about first impressions, your own and the experts'.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The soaring main theme of the first movement.** After a short orchestral introduction, the violin enters and soon arrives at the great main theme: a broad, soaring, lyrical melody, one of Tchaikovsky's finest. Listen for the violin singing this wide, ardent line. It is the heart of the first movement and a supreme example of the melodic-invention thread.

  2. **The Canzonetta: the songful slow movement.** The slow movement, marked "Canzonetta" (little song), is a tender, melancholy, muted song for the violin, brief and intimate. Listen for its sad, gentle lyricism, and for the way it leads directly, without a pause, into the finale. It is the emotional still center between the brilliant outer movements.

  3. **The Russian dance finale.** The finale bursts in with a fiery, foot-stamping Russian dance energy, full of folk character and dazzling violin display. Listen for the wild dance rhythms, the sense of a Russian festival, and the sheer virtuosity demanded of the soloist. This is the folk-dance Tchaikovsky (Chapters 6, 11, 12) in concerto form.

#### One-sentence background

The Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 35, was composed in 1878 at Clarens, Switzerland, during the recovery from the marriage crisis; rejected as unplayable by Leopold Auer, premiered by Adolph Brodsky in Vienna in 1881 (where Eduard Hanslick savaged it), it is now one of the most-loved violin concertos in the repertoire; it has three movements (a broad first movement, a songful Canzonetta leading without a break into a Russian-dance finale), runs about thirty-three minutes, and the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Jascha Heifetz with Fritz Reiner and the Chicago Symphony on RCA (1957), a famous account of dazzling brilliance; for a warmer, more Russian reading, the alternatives below apply.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Tchaikovsky Violin Concerto Heifetz Reiner Chicago" or "Tchaikovsky Violin Concerto Op. 35" ZH: "柴可夫斯基 小提琴协奏曲 海菲茨 莱纳 芝加哥" 或 "柴可夫斯基 D大调小提琴协奏曲 作品35" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* David Oistrakh in any of his recordings, the great Russian violinist's warm, broad, idiomatic reading, a benchmark of the Russian tradition.
* Itzhak Perlman with a leading orchestra, a rich, generous modern account; or Hilary Hahn or Janine Jansen for more recent, well-reviewed readings.

A note on cadenzas and editions: many violinists play the concerto in the edition and with the cadenza associated with Leopold Auer (who, having relented, edited it), which differs slightly from Tchaikovsky's original in some passages. Others play closer to the original. The differences are small; mention is made so that, if you compare recordings, you know why a passage may sound slightly different.

### 13.3 The Listening Window

This concerto's timings vary widely from recording to recording (the Heifetz / Reiner account is famously fast, well under most others), so the markers below are structural positions, not clock times; find each by ear from the landmark described.

#### First movement: Allegro moderato

**The orchestral introduction.** A brief, gentle orchestral opening sets the scene before the soloist enters.

**The violin's entrance and the main theme.** The violin enters and soon arrives at the great soaring main theme. Listen for the broad, ardent, lyrical line, the heart of the movement.

**The second theme and brilliant figuration.** A more delicate second theme appears, and the violin's writing grows more brilliant and decorative. Listen for the alternation of song and display.

**The development and the cadenza.** The themes are developed; then comes the cadenza, the solo passage for the violinist alone, showing off both lyricism and virtuosity. Listen for the violin unaccompanied, exploring the themes.

**The return and close.** The main theme returns and the movement drives to a brilliant conclusion. This first movement is by far the concerto's largest, longer than the other two combined.

#### Second movement: Canzonetta (Andante)

**The muted, songful melody.** The Canzonetta opens with a tender, melancholy, muted melody for the violin, brief and intimate. Register the gentle sadness, the song-like quality. This is the concerto's still center.

**The transition to the finale.** The Canzonetta does not end with a full stop; listen for it to lead directly, without a pause, into the finale. The slow movement's melancholy is swept away by the dance that follows.

#### Third movement: Finale (Allegro vivacissimo)

**The Russian dance.** The finale bursts in with a fiery, foot-stamping Russian dance, full of folk energy and dazzling violin writing. Listen for the wild rhythms and the festival character.

**The contrasting lyrical theme.** A broader, more songful theme contrasts with the dance, giving the ear a rest before the dance returns. Listen for the alternation of folk fire and lyrical breadth.

**The brilliant close.** The dance energy builds to a blazing, virtuosic conclusion, the violin and orchestra driving together to the end. The finale is a tour de force, and the concerto ends in triumph.

### 13.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### The concerto as melodic vehicle

Where the First Piano Concerto is heroic drama (Chapter 12), the Violin Concerto is, above all, a melodic vehicle: a showcase for the violin's unique capacity to sing. The instrument's closeness to the human voice, its ability to sustain a long, expressive, vocal line, suits Tchaikovsky's melodic gift perfectly, and the concerto's great moments are its melodies: the soaring first-movement theme, the tender Canzonetta. The violin sings these as no other instrument could, and the concerto is, in a sense, a celebration of the violin as the supreme singing instrument of the orchestra.

This is why the "unplayable" charge is so ironic in hindsight. What Auer found awkward, and what made the concerto new, was precisely Tchaikovsky's demand that the violin both sing his long Romantic melodies and execute brilliant, difficult passagework, often in unusual ways. He was asking the instrument to do, gratefully and beautifully, things slightly outside the established technique of 1878. Once violinists learned how (and Auer's own teaching showed them), the difficulty vanished into apparent naturalness, and the concerto became a byword for grateful, idiomatic violin writing. For Thread B, register the soaring first-movement theme as the concerto member of the melodic-invention family, alongside the Romeo and Juliet love-theme (Chapter 4) and the white-swan adagio (Chapter 7). Chapter 18 §18.1 collects them.

#### The Russian-dance finale

The finale brings back the folk-dance Tchaikovsky we have met in the "Little Russian" Symphony (Chapter 6), the Fourth Symphony's finale (Chapter 11), and the First Piano Concerto (Chapter 12). Its foot-stamping energy and folk character root the cosmopolitan concerto in Russian soil, the same synthesis at work throughout: Russian material in a universal form. The finale's combination of folk dance and virtuoso display makes it one of the most exciting in the concerto repertoire, and it is the moment when the Violin Concerto, like so much of Tchaikovsky, shows its Russian heart inside its international body.

### 13.5 The Middle-Period Band: 1880-1885

The Violin Concerto opens a period of settled, abundant production. Freed by von Meck's patronage from teaching, Tchaikovsky in the early 1880s produced a remarkable range of works. Five are worth knowing, and together they show the full breadth of his art. Treat this section as a guided tour rather than a deep anchor; each work rewards a listen, and you can return to any of them later.

#### The 1812 Overture, Op. 49 (1880)

The most famous and the least characteristic. The _1812 Overture_ commemorates Russia's defeat of Napoleon's invasion in 1812, and it is a deliberately spectacular crowd-pleaser, complete with cannon fire and church bells in its climax, pitting the French national anthem (the _Marseillaise_) against Russian themes in a programmatic battle that ends in Russian triumph. It was written for a grand public occasion and is pure spectacle. Tchaikovsky himself thought little of it; he called it loud and noisy and without much artistic merit. It is worth hearing once, for the spectacle and the fame, but do not mistake it for the real Tchaikovsky; it is the bombastic caricature he is unfairly reduced to, and the rest of this book is the corrective. Listen to it, enjoy the cannons, and then turn to the Serenade for Strings to hear the composer he actually was.

#### The Serenade for Strings in C major, Op. 48 (1880)

Tchaikovsky's own favorite among these works, and a perfect foil to the _1812_. The Serenade is for strings alone, and it opens with a deliberate, loving homage to Mozart, a broad, hymn-like introduction in the eighteenth-century manner (the classical Tchaikovsky of the Rococo Variations, Chapter 8). It contains a famous, lilting waltz second movement, a tender elegy, and a finale based on Russian folk tunes. The whole work is elegant, warm, and beautifully made, the antithesis of the _1812_ 's noise. If you hear one work from this section, make it the Serenade; it shows the classical, lyrical, intimate Tchaikovsky at his most lovable. The waltz alone is one of his most beguiling creations.

#### The Piano Trio in A minor, Op. 50 (1881-82)

The narrative payoff to Chapter 12. When Nikolai Rubinstein, the man who had supposedly rejected the First Piano Concerto so brutally, died in Paris in early 1881, Tchaikovsky wrote a large piano trio in his memory, subtitled "in memory of a great artist." It is a substantial, deeply felt work in two big movements, the second a set of variations, full of grief and grandeur. That the trio memorializes the very man of the famous rejection story is the proof, promised in Chapter 12, that the relationship was one of deep love beneath the quarrel. The Piano Trio is Tchaikovsky's major chamber work after the string quartets, and it is his great elegy; hear it to feel the depth of the Rubinstein friendship and to encounter the more private, chamber-scaled Tchaikovsky.

#### The Capriccio Italien, Op. 45 (1880)

A bright, sunny holiday piece, born of Tchaikovsky's travels in Italy, weaving together Italian folk and popular tunes he heard in Rome into a brilliant, colorful orchestral showpiece. It is lighter fare than the symphonies, but charming and expertly made, the sound of a man enjoying the Italian sunshine after the Russian dark. Hear it for the pure pleasure and for the orchestral color.

#### The Manfred Symphony in B minor, Op. 58 (1885)

The most ambitious of the band, and a hidden giant. The _Manfred Symphony_ is a large, four-movement programmatic symphony on Byron's dramatic poem _Manfred_ , about a tortured, guilt-haunted hero wandering the Alps, suggested to Tchaikovsky by Mily Balakirev (the same Balakirev who guided Romeo and Juliet, Chapter 4). It is sometimes called Tchaikovsky's "seventh symphony," and at about fifty-five minutes it is his longest. A recurring "Manfred" theme (an _idée fixe_ , a fixed idea representing the hero) threads through all four movements, the programmatic-narrative and recurring-motto techniques (Thread B) at their most extended. The first movement paints Manfred's tormented wandering; the finale includes a startling organ entrance. _Manfred_ is uneven and demanding, but it is a major work, far greater than the _1812_ , and it is the most direct descendant of Romeo and Juliet's programmatic ambition. For listeners who love the tone-poem side of Tchaikovsky, it is the great discovery of the middle period. Chapter 18 §18.2 collects the programmatic works, _Manfred_ among the most important.

### 13.6 The Stories

#### Two rejected concertos, one lesson

The Violin Concerto and the First Piano Concerto are twins in their reception: both rejected by the soloist they were meant for, both triumphant elsewhere, both now central to the repertoire. The pairing teaches the deepest lesson about first reactions. Auer was the greatest violin teacher of his age; Hanslick was the most powerful critic; Nikolai Rubinstein was one of the great pianists. All three found fault with concertos that the world now adores. They were not stupid or malicious; they were applying the standards of their moment to music that was slightly ahead of it, asking new things of players and listeners. The new always sounds difficult, awkward, even offensive, until the ear catches up. The most appealing music of one generation was the strange music of the one before. Hold this against your own first impressions: a work that puzzles you on first hearing may be one your ear has not yet learned, not one without worth.

#### The breadth of the middle period

The five middle-period works, heard together, are an argument against every reduction of Tchaikovsky to a single thing. The _1812 Overture_ is bombastic spectacle; the Serenade for Strings is Mozartian elegance; the Piano Trio is private grief; the _Capriccio Italien_ is sunny holiday color; the _Manfred Symphony_ is dark programmatic ambition. One composer, in a few years, wrote all five. The man reduced to "sentimental Russian" or "bombastic crowd-pleaser" was, in fact, capable of the whole range, from the noisiest public gesture to the most intimate elegy, from classical poise to Romantic torment. The middle-period band is the place to feel that range, and to put away, once and for all, the caricatures. Listen to the _1812_ and the Serenade back to back, and you will never again think you know what "a Tchaikovsky piece" sounds like.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

To the Symphonies 5 and 6 (Chapter 14). The Violin Concerto and the middle-period band lead directly to the late symphonic peak; _Manfred_ (1885) sits just before the Fifth Symphony (1888).

To the programmatic and recurring-motto threads (Chapter 18 §18.2). _Manfred_ , with its _idée fixe_ threading all four movements, is among the most extended examples, alongside Romeo and Juliet (Chapter 4) and the Fate symphonies (Chapters 11, 14).

To the classical Tchaikovsky. The Serenade for Strings joins the Rococo Variations (Chapter 8) as the great example of the Mozart-loving, classically poised composer who balances the tragic Romantic.

What does the breadth do to the caricature of Tchaikovsky as one kind of composer?"_
* _"The _Manfred Symphony_ , sometimes called Tchaikovsky's 'seventh symphony,' threads a single 'Manfred' theme through all four movements to depict a tortured wandering hero. If you sample its first movement, listen for that recurring theme. How does a recurring idea representing a character (an _idée fixe_) differ from the Fate motif of the Fourth Symphony? What does each technique let Tchaikovsky do?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Heifetz / Reiner recording (or an alternative from §13.2) and listen again to the Violin Concerto, attending to these moments:

  1. **The soaring main theme (first movement).** Soon after the violin enters, confirm the broad, ardent, lyrical line it sings, the concerto member of Tchaikovsky's melodic-invention family.

  2. **The Canzonetta (second movement).** Listen for the tender, muted, melancholy little song, the concerto's still center, and for how it leads without a pause into the finale.

  3. **The Russian dance (finale).** Listen for the fiery, foot-stamping folk energy and the dazzling violin display, the folk-dance Tchaikovsky in concerto form.

  4. **From the middle-period band, the Serenade for Strings opening.** Listen for the broad, Mozart-homage hymn that opens the Serenade, the classical Tchaikovsky, and contrast it in your mind with the cannons of the _1812_.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : hear the _1812 Overture_ and the Serenade for Strings back to back, about forty-five minutes. The two were written the same year, 1880, by the same man, and they could not be more different: the one all cannons and spectacle, the other all elegance and song. The pairing is the single best demonstration of Tchaikovsky's range and the strongest cure for any caricature of him. Decide which is the "real" Tchaikovsky, and then realize that both are.

**Read** : the chapters on the Violin Concerto and the middle-period works in Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (2009) or David Brown's biography. Both discuss the Auer rejection, the Hanslick review, and the range of the early-1880s output, and both treat the _Manfred Symphony_ as the major and underrated work it is. Reading them will set up Chapter 14, where the last two symphonies bring the symphonic story, and the question of Fate and affirmation, to its culmination.

## Chapter 14: Symphony No. 5 and Symphony No. 6 "Pathétique" — Revisited

The last two symphonies are the summit of Tchaikovsky's art and the place where the deepest question in his music comes to a head: can the human spirit win against Fate, and if it cannot, what kind of music tells the truth about that? The Fifth Symphony answers with a triumphant major-key finale that Tchaikovsky himself suspected was insincere. The Sixth, the "Pathétique," gives the opposite answer: it abandons the triumphant finale altogether and ends with a slow movement that sinks, exhausted, into silence. Between them, these two symphonies stage the great argument of Tchaikovsky's life, and the Sixth, premiered nine days before his death, has become inseparable from the legend of that death, a legend this book has been preparing you to weigh.

You met the Sixth's finale in B159, Chapter 20, where the descending line that fades to nothing was singled out as a listening lesson. Now we hear both late symphonies in the full context of Tchaikovsky's life and art, and we confront, carefully, the temptation to read the Sixth as a suicide note, a temptation that Chapter 21 will examine and that the modern scholarship resists.

### 14.1 The Fifth Symphony and its troubled triumph

Tchaikovsky composed the Fifth Symphony in 1888 and conducted its premiere in St. Petersburg in November of that year, part of the conducting career that increasingly occupied his last years. Like the Fourth, the Fifth is a Fate symphony, built around a recurring motto, but its design is even more unified: the motto, a dark, brooding theme introduced at the very start by the clarinets, returns in all four movements, not just the outer two. The symphony is the journey of this motto, from its grim opening through transformation, toward a finale in which it returns in a blazing major key, transformed from Fate into apparent victory.

And here is the trouble. Tchaikovsky did not trust the Fifth's triumphant ending. In letters, he expressed doubts about the symphony, calling it a failure, suspecting it of insincerity, of something forced and false, especially in the major-key triumph of the finale. He worried that the affirmation was willed rather than earned, a manufactured happy ending rather than a genuine one. The contemporary reception was mixed, which deepened his doubts.

Whether the Fifth's triumph is sincere is a question listeners still argue, and it is one of the most interesting questions in Tchaikovsky. Some hear the finale's major-key blaze as a genuine, hard-won victory, Fate conquered, the human spirit triumphant. Others hear exactly what Tchaikovsky feared: a forced, hollow, too-loud affirmation, protesting too much, a victory that does not convince. The truth may be that the ambiguity is the point: that Tchaikovsky, the composer of shadowed triumphs (recall the willed joy of the Fourth's finale, Chapter 11), wrote a finale whose triumph is genuinely uncertain, neither clearly sincere nor clearly false. Listen to it yourself and decide. The fact that the composer himself could not decide is part of what makes the Fifth so fascinating, and it sets up the Sixth, where he solved the problem by refusing triumph altogether.

### 14.2 Before You Listen: the two late symphonies

#### Hook Anecdote

The morning after the premiere of the Sixth Symphony in October 1893, Tchaikovsky and his brother Modest were discussing what to call it. The symphony needed a subtitle; "Programme Symphony" had been considered and rejected. Modest suggested a Russian word, "Патетическая" (_Pateticheskaya_), and Tchaikovsky seized on it. In English it became "Pathétique," and here a translation pitfall has distorted the work for English-speaking listeners ever since.

The Russian word, like the French "pathétique," does not mean "pathetic" in the modern English sense of feeble or self-pitying. It means passionate, impassioned, full of deep and serious emotion, in the older sense of "pathos" as profound feeling. The Sixth is not a self-pitying symphony; it is an impassioned one, a work of the deepest and most serious emotion. The English title misleads, suggesting weakness where the Russian means intensity. Hold the right sense in mind: this is the Passionate Symphony, the Impassioned Symphony, not the Self-Pitying one. The distinction matters, because the false sense feeds the false legend of a broken man writing his own requiem, and the true sense points to what the symphony actually is: a major artist's most powerful and considered statement, dedicated, by the way, not to death but to a living young man he loved, his nephew Vladimir "Bob" Davydov.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The Fifth's motto, transformed across four movements.** In the Fifth Symphony, listen for the dark, brooding motto theme that the clarinets introduce at the very start, and then listen for it to return in each movement: grim at first, then intruding on the lovely slow movement, glimpsed in the waltz, and finally transformed into a major-key blaze in the finale. Track this one theme through the whole symphony; its transformation from darkness to (apparent) triumph is the symphony's drama.

  2. **The Fifth's slow movement and its great horn solo.** The second movement of the Fifth opens with one of the most famous melodies Tchaikovsky wrote: a long, ardent, noble tune for solo horn over hushed strings. Listen for the horn's entrance and the warmth of the melody. It is the lyrical heart of the symphony, and the motto theme will twice intrude on its serenity, like Fate breaking into a moment of peace.

  3. **The Sixth's finale that dies into silence.** In the Sixth Symphony, listen for the finale (the fourth and last movement): not a fast, triumphant close but a slow, grieving, descending movement (marked _Adagio lamentoso_) that sinks lower and lower and fades, at the end, into near-silence, the music dying away to nothing. This reversal, ending a symphony with a slow lament instead of a triumph, is the Sixth's revolutionary stroke and the source of its devastating effect.

#### One-sentence background

The Symphony No. 5 in E minor, Op. 64 (1888), is a Fate symphony built on a motto theme recurring in all four movements and ending in a major-key triumph Tchaikovsky himself doubted; the Symphony No. 6 in B minor, "Pathétique," Op. 74 (1893), dedicated to his nephew Bob Davydov and premiered nine days before his death, reverses symphonic convention by ending with a slow lament that dies into silence; the **Primary Recording** for both is Yevgeny Mravinsky with the Leningrad Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon (1960), the benchmark account of the late symphonies. (The "Pathétique" title means "impassioned," not "self-pitying.")

#### Search Hints

EN: "Tchaikovsky Symphony No. 5 Mravinsky Leningrad" / "Tchaikovsky Symphony No. 6 Pathétique Mravinsky" ZH: "柴可夫斯基 第五交响曲 穆拉文斯基" 或 "柴可夫斯基 第六交响曲 悲怆 穆拉文斯基" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon (multiple cycles), powerful and polished.
* Mariss Jansons with the Oslo Philharmonic on Chandos, or Leonard Bernstein with the New York Philharmonic, both well-reviewed; Bernstein's late Sixth in particular is famously slow and intense.

### 14.3 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Mravinsky / Leningrad recordings. We focus on the Fifth's motto and slow movement and the Sixth's finale, the chapter's central moments.

#### Symphony No. 5: the motto and the slow movement

**First movement, 0:00. The motto.** The clarinets, low and dark, introduce the brooding motto theme over soft strings. Register it; this is the Fate idea that will run through the whole symphony. The main Allegro then grows out of this grim opening.

**Second movement, 0:00. The horn solo.** After a few solemn string chords, the solo horn enters with the great noble melody. Listen for the warmth and breadth of this famous tune, the lyrical heart of the symphony.

**Second movement, about 5:00. The motto intrudes.** Listen for the dark motto theme to break suddenly into the serene slow movement, twice, like Fate shattering a moment of peace. The contrast between the horn's warm melody and the motto's grim intrusion is the movement's drama, and it echoes the Fate interruptions of the Fourth (Chapter 11).

**Finale, the close. The motto in major.** In the finale, listen for the motto theme to return transformed into a triumphant major key, broad and blazing, as the symphony drives to its (contested) victorious close. Ask yourself, as Tchaikovsky did: does this triumph convince, or does it protest too much?

#### Symphony No. 6 "Pathétique": the finale

**Fourth movement, 0:00. The descending lament.** The finale opens with a descending, grieving string melody (a melody famously created by interweaving two violin parts, so that no single section plays the tune as written, though the ear hears one continuous descending line). Register the immediate sense of grief and falling. This is the _Adagio lamentoso_.

**About 2:00. The lament intensifies.** The grieving music builds to passionate, anguished climaxes and then subsides, surges and falls back, the emotion deepening. There is no escape into a fast triumphant movement; this slow lament is the whole finale.

**About 6:00. The sinking toward silence.** The music descends lower and lower, the energy draining, a stroke on the tam-tam (a gong) marking a moment of darkness. The strings sink into their lowest register.

**About 9:00. The dying close.** Listen for the very end: the music fades, lower and quieter, the low strings throbbing like a failing heartbeat, until the symphony dies away into near-silence. There is no final chord of triumph, no resolution into the light, only the music ebbing to nothing. It is one of the most devastating endings in all music, and it ends the symphony, and Tchaikovsky's symphonic career, in darkness. (Cross-reference: this is the finale singled out in B159, Chapter 20.)

### 14.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### The motto symphony perfected (the Fifth)

The Fifth Symphony perfects the cyclic-motto technique we have tracked from Romeo and Juliet (Chapter 4) through the Fourth (Chapter 11). Where the Fourth brought its Fate fanfare back only in the finale, the Fifth threads its motto through all four movements, so that the whole symphony is the story of a single theme's transformation. The motto is dark and grim at the start; it intrudes on the slow movement like Fate breaking into peace; it shadows the waltz; and it returns in the finale transformed into a major-key blaze. This is the most thoroughly unified of Tchaikovsky's symphonies, the motto-symphony idea taken to its logical end: one theme, transformed across forty-five minutes, carrying the entire dramatic argument. For Thread B, the Fifth's motto is the culminating example of the recurring-motto technique. Chapter 18 §18.2 collects them.

The transformation is concrete and audible, and worth pinning down. At the start the motto creeps in low in the clarinets, in E minor, moving in a slow, heavy, funeral-march tread. In the finale the same intervals return in E major, at a confident, upright march tempo: the notes are nearly unchanged, but the mode has flipped from minor to major and the gait from funeral to parade. This is not decoration; it is the symphony's whole argument in a single gesture, grief's own theme forced into the light. And it is exactly why the sincerity question, below, is so sharp. When you doubt the Fifth's triumph, you are doubting whether a theme born in the minor can be made to mean victory simply by being recast in the major and played loudly enough; the "forcing" Tchaikovsky feared is something you can actually hear being done to the motto.

#### The reversed symphony (the Sixth)

The Sixth Symphony's revolutionary stroke is structural: it reverses the conventional order of feeling in a symphony. Normally a symphony ends with its fastest, most triumphant movement, the finale as victory and resolution. The Sixth puts its most energetic, march-like movement third (a brilliant march-scherzo that builds to so triumphant a climax that audiences often applaud, thinking the symphony has ended), and then follows it not with a triumphant finale but with a slow, grieving _Adagio lamentoso_ that sinks into silence. The expected shape, struggle leading to triumph, is inverted: apparent triumph (the third movement) leading to collapse and lament (the finale).

This inversion is the Sixth's genius and its meaning. By placing the triumphant-sounding movement third and ending with a lament, Tchaikovsky says, structurally, that the triumph was false, that what looked like victory gives way to grief and exhaustion and, finally, silence. It is the answer to the question the Fifth could not resolve: the Fifth ended with a triumph Tchaikovsky did not trust; the Sixth refuses triumph altogether and ends in honest darkness. After a lifetime of shadowed, willed affirmations (the Fourth, the Fifth), Tchaikovsky finally wrote a symphony that does not pretend to win. That is what makes the Sixth feel so true, and so final.

#### The sincerity question, resolved in darkness

The deepest thread connecting the two symphonies is the question of sincere versus willed affirmation, which we first met in the Fourth (Chapter 11). Tchaikovsky's major-key triumphs always carried a suspicion of being forced; he himself voiced that suspicion about the Fifth. The Sixth resolves the tension not by achieving a sincere triumph but by abandoning triumph. Its honesty is the honesty of refusing the consoling lie. Whether or not one connects this to Tchaikovsky's death (and Chapter 21 urges caution), the Sixth is, as a piece of music, the work in which Tchaikovsky stopped manufacturing happy endings and let a symphony end in truthful grief. That artistic decision, not any biographical theory, is the real source of the symphony's overwhelming power.

### 14.5 The Stories

#### The death, and the danger of reading it backward

The Sixth Symphony was premiered in St. Petersburg on October 16 / 28, 1893, conducted by Tchaikovsky himself. Nine days later, on October 25 / November 6, he was dead, at fifty-three, of what was recorded as cholera. The closeness of the premiere and the death has made it almost irresistible to hear the Sixth as Tchaikovsky's conscious farewell, his own requiem, even his suicide note, especially given the false legend (examined in Chapter 21) that he killed himself.

This book asks you to resist the backward reading, for reasons Chapter 21 develops in full. The danger is the one we have been guarding against since Chapter 8: reading the music as autobiography, letting a biographical event become the key to a work. The Sixth was composed over months in 1893; its slow-lament finale was a considered artistic decision, the resolution of the sincerity problem that had occupied Tchaikovsky since the Fourth, not a coded announcement of an intention to die. Tchaikovsky did not know, when he wrote and premiered the Sixth, that he had nine days to live; he was making conducting plans, planning future works. The leading scholars, Roland John Wiley and Alexander Poznansky, read the finale as a compositional choice, the logical end of the motto-symphony and sincerity questions, not as a death-message. The Sixth is devastating because of what it is as music, an honest symphony that ends in grief, not because of what happened to its composer nine days after he conducted it. Hear it as the masterpiece it is; do not let the legend tell you what it means.

#### "Pathétique," once more

The translation pitfall deserves a final word, because it compounds the backward reading. The English "Pathétique" suggests self-pity and weakness; combined with the suicide legend, it produces the false image of a broken, defeated man whimpering his way to the grave. Strip both away. The Russian word means impassioned and profound; the dedication is to a living young man Tchaikovsky loved; the finale is a considered artistic decision; and the composer was, in autumn 1893, an internationally celebrated artist at the height of his powers, not a wreck. The Sixth is the impassioned, serious, structurally daring masterpiece of a great composer in full command, who chose, for once, to end a symphony in honest darkness. That is a far greater and truer thing than the legend allows.

#### Cross-piece links

To B159, Chapter 20, where the Sixth's finale was first introduced as a listening lesson. This chapter places it in the full context of Tchaikovsky's symphonic development.

To the Fourth Symphony (Chapter 11), the other great Fate symphony and the first appearance of the sincerity question, which the Fifth deepens and the Sixth resolves.

To Chapter 21, the death chapter, where the legend the Sixth has attracted is examined directly through the three positions on the cause-of-death dispute, with no verdict.

To the recurring-motto and melodic-invention threads (Chapter 18 §§18.1-18.2), where the Fifth's motto and the slow movements of both symphonies are collected.

Listen to the finale's major-key blaze and decide for yourself: does it sound like a genuine, earned victory, or like a willed, hollow one that protests too much? Is the ambiguity itself the point?"_
* _"The Sixth Symphony reverses convention by putting its triumphant-sounding movement third and ending with a slow lament that dies into silence. Listen to the third movement's exciting climax (which often draws premature applause) and then to the grieving finale. Why is ending with a lament instead of a triumph so devastating? What does the inversion say that a conventional order could not?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Mravinsky / Leningrad recordings (or alternatives from §14.2) and listen again, attending to these moments:

  1. **The Fifth's motto, traced through the symphony.** Listen for the dark clarinet motto at the start (first movement), its intrusion into the slow movement (around 5:00 of the second movement), and its major-key transformation in the finale. Track the one theme across all four movements.

  2. **The Fifth's horn solo (second movement, 0:00).** Confirm the great noble melody for solo horn over hushed strings, the lyrical heart of the symphony, and notice how the motto twice shatters its peace.

  3. **The Sixth's descending lament (finale, 0:00).** Listen for the grieving, descending string melody that opens the _Adagio lamentoso_. Register the immediate sense of falling and grief.

  4. **The Sixth's dying close (finale, around 9:00).** The most devastating moment in Tchaikovsky. Listen for the music sinking lower and quieter, the low strings throbbing, until the symphony fades into near-silence. No triumph, no resolution, only honest darkness.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : hear the finales of the Fifth and the Sixth back to back, about twenty-five minutes. The Fifth ends in a major-key triumph its composer distrusted; the Sixth ends in a slow lament that dies into silence. The two endings are the two answers to the question that haunted Tchaikovsky's symphonies, can the spirit win against Fate, and hearing them together, you feel the whole arc of his struggle with that question, from forced triumph to honest grief. It is the single most revealing comparison in his symphonic output.

**Read** : the chapters on the Fifth and Sixth Symphonies in Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (2009), which discuss the sincerity question, the "Pathétique" title, and, crucially, the case against reading the Sixth as a death-message. Wiley and Alexander Poznansky are the essential modern voices on the relationship between the Sixth and the death, and reading them is the best possible preparation for Chapter 21, where the cause-of-death dispute is examined directly. Resist, until then, any account that tells you the Sixth means the death; hear it first as music.

## Chapter 15: The Sleeping Beauty, Op. 66

Twelve years after the troubled birth of _Swan Lake_ , Tchaikovsky returned to the ballet, and this time everything was different. He had a great choreographer as a living collaborator, Marius Petipa, the master of the imperial ballet, who handed him a scenario of extraordinary precision, specifying the character, tempo, and even the number of bars for each number. He had a worthy theatre, the Mariinsky in St. Petersburg, and a production equal to the music. And he had, by his own account, the deepest affection for the result: _The Sleeping Beauty_ was Tchaikovsky's favorite among his three ballets, the one he loved best of all. It is the grandest and most lavish of them, a three-hour fairy-tale spectacle of waltzes, fairy variations, and processional splendor, and it shows the symphonic ballet, invented in _Swan Lake_ , brought to full maturity in collaboration with the greatest choreographer of the age.

This chapter anchors on the most famous music in the ballet: the great Act I Waltz, the Rose Adagio, the Lilac Fairy theme that is the work's benevolent thread, and the wedding-suite of Act III. It develops the principle of §5.T3, ballet as serious orchestral music, by showing how a detailed choreographer's brief, far from cramping Tchaikovsky, drew from him some of his richest orchestral characterization.

### 15.1 The Petipa collaboration

The making of _The Sleeping Beauty_ is one of the great collaborations in music, and it overturns a romantic assumption about how composers should work. Marius Petipa, the French-born choreographer who had dominated the St. Petersburg imperial ballet for decades, did not simply ask Tchaikovsky for ballet music and leave him free. He provided a detailed plan, number by number, specifying the dramatic content, the character, the tempo, the meter, and often the exact number of measures he needed for each dance and mime scene. The composer was given a precise brief, almost an engineering specification, for the whole three-hour work.

A lesser composer might have found this stifling. Tchaikovsky found it liberating. The precise requirements gave his imagination a frame to fill, and he filled it with some of his most inventive and characterful music. Working to Petipa's specifications, he produced a score in which every character has a musical identity, every scene a distinct color, the whole bound by recurring themes. The collaboration proves a point that runs counter to the myth of the solitary inspired genius: that constraint can be generative, that a clear brief from a great collaborator can free rather than fetter the imagination. _The Sleeping Beauty_ is the product of two major artists working in tight coordination, and it is the better for it.

The ballet is based on Charles Perrault's fairy tale of the princess cursed to sleep a hundred years and woken by a prince's kiss. It was premiered at the Mariinsky Theatre in St. Petersburg in January 1890, with Petipa's choreography, and it has remained a cornerstone of the classical-ballet repertoire ever since, the great test piece of a ballerina's classical technique (the Rose Adagio especially) and of a company's grandeur.

### 15.2 Before You Listen: The Sleeping Beauty

#### Hook Anecdote

Consider what it means that this, of all his works, was Tchaikovsky's favorite ballet. Not _Swan Lake_ , with its tragic love and its immortal swan theme; not _The Nutcracker_ , with its Christmas magic and its celesta. _The Sleeping Beauty_ , the grandest, most formal, most purely beautiful of the three, the fairy-tale of good triumphing over evil, light over dark, the Lilac Fairy over the wicked Carabosse. Tchaikovsky, the composer of tragic symphonies and dark operas, loved best the ballet that is a radiant fairy-tale with a happy ending.

This tells you something against the "tragic Russian" caricature. Tchaikovsky was not only, or even mainly, a man drawn to darkness. He loved beauty, elegance, splendor, the classical orderliness of the eighteenth-century court (the ballet is set in a Versailles-like fairy-tale kingdom and breathes the air of Versailles), and the affirmation of good over evil. _The Sleeping Beauty_ is his most affirmative large work, and he loved it most. When you listen, hear not a man escaping his tragic nature into mere prettiness, but a man expressing the other, equally real side of himself: the lover of radiance, order, and the triumph of the good. The Lilac Fairy's serene, generous theme is as much the true Tchaikovsky as the Fate fanfare of the Fourth Symphony.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The Lilac Fairy theme.** The good fairy of the ballet, the Lilac Fairy, who softens the curse and protects the princess, has a broad, flowing, benevolent theme that recurs throughout the work. Listen for it: serene, generous, warm, the musical symbol of protective good. It is the ballet's benevolent thread, the counterpart to the harsh music of the wicked fairy Carabosse, and it returns at every turn of the good fairy's intervention.

  2. **The great Waltz (Act I).** The Garland Waltz of Act I is one of Tchaikovsky's grandest and most famous waltzes, a broad, sweeping dance for the full court. Listen for the long, soaring waltz melody and the sense of a great formal celebration. Tchaikovsky was the supreme waltz composer of the symphonic and ballet repertoire, and this is one of his finest.

  3. **The orchestral characterization of good and evil.** Throughout, listen for how Tchaikovsky paints the opposed forces: the serene, flowing music of the Lilac Fairy and the good fairies against the harsh, grotesque, agitated music of the wicked Carabosse. Each fairy in the Prologue, too, has her own variation with its own instrumental color. This precise matching of music to character, in the service of a clear dramatic opposition, is the ballet's structural backbone.

#### One-sentence background

_The Sleeping Beauty_ , Op. 66, composed in 1888-89 to a detailed scenario by the choreographer Marius Petipa and based on Perrault's fairy tale, is the grandest of Tchaikovsky's three ballets and his own favorite; premiered at the Mariinsky Theatre in St. Petersburg in January 1890, it is built around the opposition of the benevolent Lilac Fairy and the wicked Carabosse, contains the famous Garland Waltz and the Rose Adagio, runs about three hours complete, and the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is André Previn conducting the London Symphony Orchestra in the complete ballet on EMI, chosen for its symphonic richness and its standing as a reference recording.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Tchaikovsky Sleeping Beauty Previn London Symphony" or "Tchaikovsky Sleeping Beauty complete ballet" ZH: "柴可夫斯基 睡美人 普列文 伦敦交响" 或 "柴可夫斯基 睡美人 全曲" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Antal Doráti with the Concertgebouw Orchestra on Philips, a famous complete account with great rhythmic life.
* Valery Gergiev with the Mariinsky (Kirov) Orchestra on Philips, from the house where the ballet was born, for a more overtly Russian theatrical sound.

If you do not have time for the complete three-hour ballet, listen first to the highlights: the Introduction (which opposes the Carabosse and Lilac Fairy themes), the Garland Waltz, the Rose Adagio, and the Act III wedding numbers.

### 15.3 The Listening Window

The markers below are structural landmarks within the relevant numbers, not clock times; since you may be hearing highlights or any of several complete sets, find each number by name and each moment by ear.

#### The Introduction: Carabosse versus the Lilac Fairy

**The Carabosse music.** The ballet opens with harsh, agitated, menacing music: the theme of the wicked fairy Carabosse, who will curse the infant princess. Register its grotesque, threatening character.

**The Lilac Fairy theme.** In contrast, listen for the broad, serene, flowing Lilac Fairy theme, the music of protective good. The Introduction states the work's central opposition at once: evil and good, curse and protection, in two contrasting themes. This is the dramatic frame of the whole ballet.

#### The Garland Waltz (Act I)

**The waltz begins.** The great Act I Waltz opens with a sweeping introduction and then the broad waltz melody. Listen for the long, soaring tune and the grand, formal, celebratory character. This is a court at festival, and Tchaikovsky's waltz is one of his finest.

**The waltz develops.** The melody returns and expands, the full orchestra swelling, the dance growing grander. Listen for the breadth and sweep; this is ballroom music raised to symphonic richness.

#### The Rose Adagio (Act I)

**Aurora and her suitors.** The Rose Adagio is the famous test piece: the princess Aurora dances with four suitors, each presenting her a rose, in a slow, sustained adagio of great difficulty for the ballerina. Listen for the broad, noble, sustained melody and the sense of formal courtship. The music must support long-held balances and slow turns, so it is stately and spacious.

**The adagio builds.** The melody grows in warmth and grandeur as the dance reaches its climax. Listen for the nobility and breadth; this is Tchaikovsky writing music for a princess at the height of her grace.

#### The Act III wedding suite

In Act III, the wedding of Aurora and the Prince, listen for a suite of brilliant character dances and the grand final pas de deux. Fairy-tale characters appear (Puss in Boots, the Bluebird, and others), each with a vivid character-dance, and the act builds to the grand Aurora-Prince pas de deux and a final triumphant procession. Listen for the variety of the character dances and the splendor of the close: good has triumphed, the curse is broken, and the ballet ends in radiant celebration. Find the Act III numbers, especially the famous Bluebird pas de deux and the grand finale.

### 15.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### The symphonic ballet matured

If _Swan Lake_ invented the symphonic ballet (Chapter 7), _The Sleeping Beauty_ matured it. The features are the same, recurring themes that carry meaning, orchestral seriousness, dramatic development, but they are handled with greater assurance and on a grander scale. The opposition of the Lilac Fairy and Carabosse themes frames the whole work like the conflict of themes in a symphony; the orchestral characterization is richer and more varied; the structure, across three acts and a prologue, is more ambitious. Working with Petipa's precise brief, Tchaikovsky produced a score of symphonic weight and coherence wrapped in the splendor of a fairy-tale spectacle.

The maturity shows especially in the orchestral color. Each fairy in the Prologue has her own variation with its own instrumental personality; the Lilac Fairy's serene theme contrasts with Carabosse's grotesquerie; the character dances of Act III each have a distinct sound. Tchaikovsky's gift for matching music to character, which we tracked from Romeo and Juliet (Chapter 4) through _Swan Lake_ (Chapter 7), reaches a new richness here, and it will reach its absolute peak in _The Nutcracker_ (Chapter 16). For Thread B, the orchestral-color achievement of _The Sleeping Beauty_ joins the collection in Chapter 18 §18.3.

#### The waltz as a Tchaikovsky signature

_The Sleeping Beauty_ 's Garland Waltz is a reminder that Tchaikovsky was the supreme waltz composer of the symphonic and ballet repertoire. The waltz runs through his work: the waltz movement of the Fifth Symphony (Chapter 14), the waltzes in _Swan Lake_ and _Eugene Onegin_ , the great waltzes of all three ballets. He raised the waltz, a social dance, to the level of art, giving it long, soaring melodies and rich orchestration while keeping its lilt and sweep. The Garland Waltz is one of his grandest. When you hear it, you are hearing one of the things Tchaikovsky did better than any other composer: the symphonic waltz, the ballroom dance made into great music. It is another facet of the melodic-invention thread (Chapter 18 §18.1).

#### Constraint as a creative spur

The deepest lesson of _The Sleeping Beauty_ is the one from §15.1: that Petipa's detailed constraints spurred rather than stifled Tchaikovsky's invention. This is worth holding onto, because it runs against a common romantic idea of artistic freedom. Tchaikovsky did some of his most characterful work to a tight brief, the number of bars specified, the character and tempo dictated. The constraint focused his imagination; the precise requirements became prompts. A composer asked for "thirty-two bars of menacing music for the evil fairy's entrance" produced the vivid Carabosse music; a composer asked for "a grand waltz for the court" produced the Garland Waltz. The brief did not limit the genius; it gave it shape to fill. This is a truth about creative work generally, and _The Sleeping Beauty_ is its musical demonstration.

### 15.5 The Stories

#### Why it was his favorite

Tchaikovsky's special affection for _The Sleeping Beauty_ repays thought, because it tells you what he valued. The ballet is the most affirmative, most radiant, most purely beautiful of his large works, a fairy-tale of good triumphing over evil with no shadow at the end. That the composer of the "Pathétique" loved this best suggests that the affirmative, beauty-loving side of him was as deep as the tragic side, perhaps deeper. He may have loved it because it let him express, without irony or shadow, his love of elegance, order, splendor, and the victory of the good, the things the tragic symphonies could only reach through struggle and loss. _The Sleeping Beauty_ is Tchaikovsky at peace, writing radiant beauty for its own sake, and he loved the rest it gave him from his darker preoccupations.

#### The eighteenth-century world

_The Sleeping Beauty_ is set in a fairy-tale kingdom modeled on the France of Louis XIV, and its music breathes the air of the eighteenth-century court: stately, formal, elegant, full of the dances and manners of the _ancien régime_. This is the classical Tchaikovsky again, the lover of Mozart and the eighteenth century whom we met in the Rococo Variations (Chapter 8) and the Serenade for Strings (Chapter 13). The ballet's courtly elegance is of a piece with that side of him: the Versailles setting let him indulge his love of eighteenth-century grace at full length and in full color. The character dances of Act III, with their formal courtliness, and the stately processionals, are the classical Tchaikovsky in ballet form. The man who worshipped Mozart found, in a fairy-tale set at a Versailles-like court, a perfect vehicle for his classicism.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

To _The Nutcracker_ (Chapter 16), the third and final ballet, where the orchestral characterization reaches its peak with the celesta of the Sugar Plum Fairy. _The Sleeping Beauty_ and _The Nutcracker_ are the two late ballets, both products of the Petipa collaboration (though Petipa fell ill during _The Nutcracker_), both at the Mariinsky.

To the orchestral-color thread (Chapter 18 §18.3), where the fairy variations and the Lilac Fairy / Carabosse opposition join the collection of Tchaikovsky's color achievements.

To the classical Tchaikovsky, the Rococo Variations (Chapter 8) and the Serenade for Strings (Chapter 13), whose eighteenth-century elegance the ballet's Versailles world shares.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Petipa gave Tchaikovsky a detailed brief, specifying the character, tempo, and even the number of bars for each number. Listen to the vivid Carabosse music or the Garland Waltz. Can you hear music written to so precise a specification, and does knowing the constraint diminish the result for you or not? When, in your own experience, does a clear brief spur a creator rather than limit one?"_
* _"_The Sleeping Beauty_ , the most radiant and affirmative of the three ballets, was Tchaikovsky's favorite, not the tragic _Swan Lake_ or the magical _Nutcracker_. What does this preference tell you about a composer best known for tragic symphonies? Listen to the serene Lilac Fairy theme and ask whether the beauty-loving Tchaikovsky is as 'real' as the tragic one."_
* _"The ballet opposes two themes from the start: the harsh Carabosse music and the serene Lilac Fairy theme, evil and good. Listen to the Introduction, where both appear. How does Tchaikovsky make 'good' and 'evil' audible in music? What musical qualities does he give each, and does the opposition work without the staging to explain it?"_
* _"The Garland Waltz is one of Tchaikovsky's grandest waltzes, and he was the supreme waltz composer of the symphonic repertoire. Listen to it. What does Tchaikovsky add to a social dance to make it great art, while keeping its lilt and sweep? Why might the waltz have suited his melodic gift so well?"_
* _"The ballet is set in a Versailles-like eighteenth-century court and breathes classical elegance, the same love of the eighteenth century we heard in the Rococo Variations. How does this courtly, formal world differ from the Russian-landscape Tchaikovsky of the early symphonies? Which feels more central to him, the Russian melancholy or the classical elegance, and can a composer be equally both?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Previn / LSO recording (or an alternative from §15.2) and listen again, attending to these moments:

  1. **The Carabosse and Lilac Fairy themes (Introduction).** Confirm the harsh, menacing Carabosse music and the broad, serene Lilac Fairy theme. Register the opposition of evil and good that frames the whole ballet.

  2. **The Garland Waltz (Act I).** Listen for the long, soaring waltz melody and the grand, celebratory sweep, one of Tchaikovsky's finest symphonic waltzes.

  3. **The Rose Adagio (Act I).** Listen for the broad, noble, sustained melody that supports the ballerina's slow, difficult dance with her four suitors. Register the stately spaciousness.

  4. **The Act III wedding numbers.** Find the character dances (especially the Bluebird pas de deux) and the grand finale. Listen for the variety of color and the radiant, triumphant close: good has won, the curse is broken.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : hear the Introduction (with its Carabosse-versus-Lilac-Fairy opposition) and then the same two themes wherever they recur in the ballet. The exercise, depending on how much you sample, trains your ear to follow recurring character-themes across a three-hour work, the ballet version of the symphonic motto-tracking you practiced in the Fourth and Fifth Symphonies, and it prepares you for the orchestral-color convergence in Chapter 18.

**Read** : Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky's Ballets_ (Oxford), the definitive scholarly study of the three ballets, which discusses the Petipa collaboration and the detailed scenario in depth, or the ballet chapters in his 2009 biography. Wiley is the leading authority on exactly how Petipa and Tchaikovsky worked together, and reading him will deepen your appreciation of _The Nutcracker_ in Chapter 16, where the collaboration continued under more difficult circumstances.
