## Chapter 19: Recording History — the Tchaikovsky Performance Tradition

Tchaikovsky is one of the most-recorded composers in history, and his major works exist in hundreds of recordings stretching back more than a century. This chapter is a map of that territory, organized by genre, naming the lineages and the landmark accounts so that you can build a Tchaikovsky collection and hear how different performers realize the voice that Chapter 18 described. It is a practical chapter, a guide to where the music lives on record, and it follows the recording-hallucination safeguard this book has used throughout: every recording named here is either at least a decade old or has been widely reviewed, so that you are pointed toward real, findable, time-tested accounts rather than to the latest release.

A note on how to use this chapter. You do not need to own many recordings; one good account of each major work is enough to start. But comparing two readings of the same work, a Russian-school account and a Western one, say, is one of the most instructive things a listener can do, because it reveals how much of what you hear is the performer's choice rather than the composer's fixed text. The chapter is organized so that you can find a reliable first recording of any major work, and then, if you wish, a contrasting second.

### 19.1 The symphonies

The benchmark for the late symphonies (the Fourth, Fifth, and Sixth) is the Russian school, and above all Yevgeny Mravinsky with the Leningrad Philharmonic, whose recordings of the last three symphonies, made in 1960 (in London and Vienna, on Deutsche Grammophon), have remained a reference for more than sixty years. Mravinsky brings a ferocious intensity, a dark-toned string sound, a brass of frightening power, and a structural tautness that makes the case, as no lush reading can, that these are rigorous symphonic dramas, not sentimental effusions. We have used Mravinsky as our benchmark throughout this book, and for the last three symphonies he remains the first recommendation, the Russian tradition speaking from inside.

The Western tradition offers a different and equally valid approach. Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic recorded the late symphonies several times across the 1960s, 70s, and 80s on Deutsche Grammophon, bringing a polished, powerful, beautifully blended orchestral sound, more refined and less raw than Mravinsky, the music's grandeur emphasized over its ferocity. Leonard Bernstein with the New York Philharmonic offers a more theatrical, emotionally extravagant reading, his late recording of the Sixth famously slow and intense. Mariss Jansons recorded a complete cycle with the Oslo Philharmonic on Chandos in the late 1980s, well-reviewed and clear, which we used as our reference for the early symphonies (the First, Second, and Third), where Mravinsky did not record.

For the early symphonies, in addition to Jansons, Mikhail Pletnev with the Russian National Orchestra (Deutsche Grammophon, 1990s) offers sharply characterized, idiomatically Russian readings of the complete cycle, and Antal Doráti with the London Symphony Orchestra (Mercury, 1960s) gives vivid, rhythmically alive vintage accounts. Any of these complete cycles will give you all six symphonies plus the _Manfred_ in consistent, reliable readings.

### 19.2 The ballets

For the three ballets, complete recordings are the goal, since the suites give only the highlights. André Previn with the London Symphony Orchestra (EMI, 1970s) recorded all three complete with symphonic richness and polish, and we have used him as our ballet benchmark throughout. Antal Doráti recorded the complete ballets (with the Concertgebouw and other orchestras, on Philips and Mercury) with great rhythmic life and theatrical energy, a classic alternative. Valery Gergiev with the Mariinsky (Kirov) Orchestra (Philips, 1990s) brings the ballets back to the St. Petersburg house where two of them were premiered, in darker, more overtly Russian and theatrical readings. For the popular numbers alone, any well-reviewed recording of the _Nutcracker Suite_ or _Swan Lake_ and _Sleeping Beauty_ highlights will serve as an introduction, but the complete ballets reward the fuller commitment.

### 19.3 The piano concertos

The First Piano Concerto's recorded history is long and starry. The classic account is Sviatoslav Richter with Herbert von Karajan and the Vienna Symphony Orchestra (Deutsche Grammophon, September 1962), our Primary Recording in Chapter 12, monumental and powerful. (Note the orchestra is the Vienna Symphony, not the Vienna Philharmonic; the two are different ensembles, and recordings are sometimes mislabeled.) Van Cliburn with Kirill Kondrashin (RCA, 1958) preserves the young American's Moscow Tchaikovsky Competition triumph in a warm, broad reading. Martha Argerich in her several recordings brings fire and brilliance. Among more recent pianists who have recorded the concerto, Lang Lang, Yuja Wang, and Daniil Trifonov have all made well-reviewed accounts; choose one with multiple reviews if you want a modern recording. For the rarer Second and Third Concertos, look for complete-concerto sets by a single pianist, several of which exist on major labels.

### 19.4 The Violin Concerto

The Violin Concerto, too, has a rich discography. Jascha Heifetz with Fritz Reiner and the Chicago Symphony (RCA, 1957), our Primary Recording in Chapter 13, is famous for its dazzling, fast, brilliant playing. David Oistrakh, the great Russian violinist, recorded it several times in warm, broad, deeply idiomatic readings that represent the Russian tradition. Itzhak Perlman offers a rich, generous modern-classic account. Among recent violinists, Hilary Hahn and Janine Jansen have made well-reviewed recordings. As Chapter 13 noted, recordings differ slightly depending on whether the violinist uses the Auer edition (with its cuts and adjustments) or plays closer to Tchaikovsky's original; the differences are small, and either way the great melodies come through.

### 19.5 The operas

For the operas, recordings from inside the Russian tradition are especially valuable, because the language and the idiom matter so much. _Eugene Onegin_ exists in many accounts; Georg Solti with the Royal Opera House (Decca, 1974), our Primary Recording in Chapter 10, is dramatically intense, and James Levine with the Staatskapelle Dresden (Deutsche Grammophon, 1987) is warm and lyrical, while Mstislav Rostropovich's Bolshoi recording (1970) offers the most idiomatically Russian sound. _The Queen of Spades_ is well served by Valery Gergiev with the Kirov (Philips, 1992), our Chapter 17 Primary Recording, idiomatic and intense, and by Rostropovich's Russian-cast account. For both operas, a Russian-language recording with native singers is worth seeking out, since the prosody and color of the Russian are part of the music.

### 19.6 How to listen comparatively

The single most instructive exercise in this chapter is to hear the same work in two contrasting readings. Take the Fourth Symphony in Mravinsky's fierce Russian account and then in Karajan's polished Western one. Take the First Piano Concerto in Richter's monumental reading and then in Argerich's fiery one. Take the Sixth Symphony in Mravinsky's taut version and then in Bernstein's extravagant late one. In each case, the notes are the same; what differs is everything else, tempo, weight, color, emotional temperature, the balance of power and refinement, and the difference teaches you how much of a performance is the performer's interpretation rather than the composer's fixed text.

This is not an idle exercise. It bears directly on the central argument of this book. The Russian-school readings, fierce, dark, structurally taut, make the case that Tchaikovsky is a rigorous master; the lush Western readings can make him sound more sentimental. How you hear Tchaikovsky depends partly on whom you hear playing him, and the choice of recording is, in a small way, a choice about which Tchaikovsky you believe in. Comparative listening is how you discover that the "sentimental Tchaikovsky" and the "rigorous Tchaikovsky" are partly artifacts of performance style, and that the music can bear, and reward, both kinds of reading.

### 19.7 The bridge to the Russian-Western question

The recording history we have just surveyed is not only a practical guide; it is itself a chapter in the larger story this book has been telling, the story of Tchaikovsky's position between Russia and the West, which Chapter 20 takes up directly. The two great performance traditions, the Russian school of Mravinsky and the Western school of Karajan, are not merely two styles of playing. They are two understandings of who Tchaikovsky was, and the tension between them is the performing echo of the deeper tension that Chapter 5 introduced and Chapter 20 will resolve: was Tchaikovsky essentially a Russian composer, or essentially a European one?

The Russian tradition hears him from the inside, as one of their own. Mravinsky's ferocity, his dark string sound, his refusal of sentimentality, his structural rigor, these are a Russian conductor's claim that Tchaikovsky is a serious, even severe, master, fully Russian and fully great, not the soft melodist the West sometimes patronized. The Russian performance tradition has been, in effect, an argument for taking Tchaikovsky seriously, made from within the culture that produced him. When Mravinsky drives the Fourth Symphony's Fate motif with frightening power, he is saying: this is not pretty music; this is a Russian master's tragic vision, and you will hear its iron.

The Western tradition hears him differently, as a great European Romantic whose music joins the international mainstream. Karajan's beautiful, polished, grand readings present Tchaikovsky as the peer of Brahms and Wagner in the European tradition, his Russianness one color among many rather than his essence. This is not a lesser view; it is the view that made Tchaikovsky the most internationally beloved of all Russian composers, the one whose music traveled furthest precisely because it was not narrowly national. The Western tradition's claim is that Tchaikovsky belongs to the world, that his appeal transcends his nationality, that he is a universal Romantic master.

Which of these is the real Tchaikovsky? The temptation is to choose a favorite and settle the matter, but the next chapter argues that this is the wrong question. The two traditions have endured side by side for generations precisely because the music answers to both: it can be played as fiercely Russian or as grandly European because something in it is genuinely each. What that doubleness means, where it places Tchaikovsky between the nationalism of the Mighty Five and the cosmopolitanism of the West, and what he passed on to the Russian composers who followed, is the subject Chapter 20 now takes up. The recordings have carried us to the threshold of the deepest question in this book: who, between Russia and the West, was he?

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter recommends hearing the same work in two contrasting readings, a fierce Russian-school account (Mravinsky) and a polished Western one (Karajan). If you can, do this with the Fourth Symphony. What specifically differs, given that the notes are identical? Which reading makes Tchaikovsky sound more 'rigorous' and which more 'sentimental,' and what does that teach about the role of performance?"_
* _"Can a composer's reputation really depend on performance style? The Russian tradition's fierce, taut readings have helped make the case that Tchaikovsky is a serious master rather than a sentimentalist. Do you find that plausible, and can you think of a work whose standing has been elevated or diminished by the way it is usually played?"_
* _"Every recording named in this chapter is at least a decade old or widely reviewed, following the book's anti-hallucination safeguard. Why is this caution useful for a listener? How would you go about verifying that a recommended recording actually exists and is well regarded before trusting a guide like this one?"_
* _"For the operas, the chapter stresses the value of Russian-language recordings with native singers, since the prosody and color of the Russian are part of the music. Listen to a scene from _Eugene Onegin_ in Russian. Even without understanding the words, can you hear how the sound of the language shapes the vocal line? What might be lost in a translated performance?"_
* _"The chapter ends by arguing that the two performance traditions, Russian and Western, are 'living proof' that Tchaikovsky was both a Russian composer and a European master. Before reading Chapter 20, where do you instinctively place him, more Russian or more European? What in the music pulls you toward each answer?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is a comparative-listening chapter. Set aside time for one side-by-side comparison:

  1. **The Fourth Symphony, two ways.** Hear the first movement in Mravinsky's Russian-school account and then in Karajan's Western one. Listen for the difference in the brass (ferocious versus grand), the strings (dark versus blended), and the overall temperature (raw versus polished).

  2. **The First Piano Concerto, two ways.** Hear the opening in Richter's monumental reading and then in Argerich's fiery one. Listen for tempo and weight.

  3. **The Sixth Symphony finale, two ways.** Hear it in Mravinsky's taut version and then in Bernstein's slow, extravagant late one. Listen for how differently the dying close can be paced.

  4. **A scene from an opera in Russian.** Hear the Tatiana Letter Scene or a scene from _The Queen of Spades_ in a Russian-language recording. Listen for how the sound of the language shapes the melody.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : choose your single favorite Tchaikovsky work and find two contrasting recordings of it, ideally one Russian-school and one Western. Listen to the same passage in both, back to back, several times. The exercise, an hour or so, will teach you more about the art of interpretation, and about the double nature of Tchaikovsky, than any amount of reading, and it directly prepares the question of Chapter 20.

**Read** : the recording-comparison writing of critics like David Hurwitz and Norman Lebrecht, who have surveyed the Tchaikovsky discography for general listeners and whose guides (in print and online) can point you to current well-reviewed accounts beyond the time-tested ones named here. Their comparative reviews model the kind of listening this chapter recommends. Reading them alongside the chapter will sharpen your ear for the differences between performances and prepare you for Chapter 20's synthesis of the Russian-Western question.

---

## Chapter 20: Tchaikovsky's Russian-Western Position

Chapter 5 introduced the question; the whole book has been gathering evidence; this chapter answers it. Where does Tchaikovsky belong? Was he a Russian composer or a European one? The answer, which the recording traditions of Chapter 19 already pointed toward, is that he was both, irreducibly, and that his greatness and his unique historical position lie exactly in the doubleness. He was the hybrid, the Western-trained Russian, who belonged fully neither to the nationalist Mighty Five nor to the German-Italian-French mainstream, and who, precisely because he stood between them, became the Russian composer the whole world adopted and the bridge by which the Western tradition and the Russian tradition met.

This chapter synthesizes the Russian-Western thread (the §5.T2 principle), traces Tchaikovsky's legacy to the Russian composers who came after him, and confronts the strange afterlife of his reputation: the Soviet appropriation that turned him into a state asset while suppressing the truth of his life, and the post-Soviet rehabilitation that has tried to recover the honest man. It introduces no new music; it gathers what you have heard into a final account of who Tchaikovsky was, historically and culturally.

### 20.1 The hybrid position, restated

Recall the opposition of Chapter 5. The Mighty Five, Mily Balakirev, Modest Mussorgsky, Alexander Borodin, Nikolai Rimsky-Korsakov, and César Cui, wanted a native Russian art music built from Russian materials on Russian principles, made by composers free of the German conservatory tradition. Tchaikovsky was the great Russian composer who stood opposite them: conservatory-trained, cosmopolitan, a worshipper of Mozart, a builder of Western symphonies, who used Russian folk material but set it inside Western structures.

The whole book has shown this hybrid at work. The folk-song integration thread (Chapter 18 §18.4) is its practical core: Tchaikovsky took Russian and Ukrainian folk tunes, kept their folk character, and set them inside sonata forms and Western harmony, in the Andante cantabile, the "Little Russian" Symphony, the First Piano Concerto, the Fourth Symphony's finale. The symphonies are Western forms filled with Russian feeling. The ballets are a French-Russian imperial art raised to symphonic seriousness. The operas set Russian literature (Pushkin) in an idiom that owes as much to Italian and French opera as to anything Russian. Everywhere, the pattern is the same: Russian substance, Western form; Russian feeling, European technique.

This was not indecision or compromise. It was a considered artistic position, and it was the right one for Tchaikovsky's gifts and temperament. He genuinely loved Russian folk material and was genuinely formed by the Western tradition, and he saw no reason to choose. The Mighty Five's nationalism and the Western academy's cosmopolitanism were, to him, two resources, not two camps demanding allegiance. He took from both and belonged wholly to neither, and the music that resulted is unmistakably his and unmistakably both Russian and European.

### 20.2 Why the hybrid traveled

The hybrid position had a consequence that shaped music history: it made Tchaikovsky the most internationally beloved of all Russian composers, the one whose music traveled furthest and fastest beyond Russia. Chapter 5 noted this; here is why.

The Mighty Five's nationalism, glorious as it was, was also a limitation on travel. Music built specifically from Russian materials on Russian principles, addressed to a Russian national project, was slower to cross borders; it could seem, to Western audiences, exotic, special, foreign, a national curiosity. Mussorgsky's _Boris Godunov_ and Borodin's _Prince Igor_ are great works, but they entered the international repertoire more slowly and more partially than Tchaikovsky's symphonies and ballets and concertos, which spoke a language the whole Western world already understood: the language of the international Romantic mainstream, inflected with a Russian accent that added color without requiring translation.

Tchaikovsky's music, because it was built on Western forms and a Western harmonic language, was immediately legible to Western audiences, while its Russian melancholy and lyricism gave it a distinctive flavor that made it more, not less, appealing. It was Russian enough to be exotic and Western enough to be familiar, the perfect combination for international success. The First Piano Concerto conquered Boston (Chapter 12); the symphonies and ballets conquered the concert halls and opera houses of Europe and America; Tchaikovsky himself toured as a celebrity conductor, opening Carnegie Hall in 1891. The outsider in Russian musical politics became the insider of the international repertoire. The hybrid position that cost him at home won him the world.

### 20.3 The legacy: the Russian composers who came after

Tchaikovsky's hybrid solution, Russian feeling in Western forms, became one of the two great models for Russian composers who followed, the other being the nationalism of the Five. The composers of the next generations divided and combined these inheritances, and Tchaikovsky's line runs through the greatest of them.

Sergei Rachmaninoff is the most direct heir. His music, the piano concertos, the symphonies, the songs, takes Tchaikovsky's long arching melody, his rich harmony, his Russian melancholy, and his Western forms, and extends them into the twentieth century. Rachmaninoff is unthinkable without Tchaikovsky; he is the continuation of the lyrical, cosmopolitan-Russian line, the melodic-invention thread (Chapter 18 §18.1) carried forward. When you hear a Rachmaninoff melody soar, you are hearing Tchaikovsky's descendant.

Igor Stravinsky is the more surprising heir, because he is so often seen as a revolutionary who broke with the past. But Stravinsky revered Tchaikovsky, defended him against the nationalists, and drew on him, most openly in the ballet _The Fairy's Kiss_ , built on Tchaikovsky's own melodies, and in his lifelong love of the eighteenth-century, Mozart-loving Tchaikovsky, the classical side we met in the Rococo Variations and the Serenade. Stravinsky's neoclassicism has a Tchaikovskian root. Even the great ballet revolution of _The Rite of Spring_ descends, through the symphonic-ballet idea, from Tchaikovsky's _Swan Lake_ and _Sleeping Beauty_ : the notion that ballet music could be serious symphonic art began with Tchaikovsky.

Dmitri Shostakovich, the great Soviet symphonist, inherited Tchaikovsky's conception of the symphony as a vehicle for tragic personal and human drama, the Fate-haunted, emotionally charged symphony of the Fourth, Fifth, and Sixth. Shostakovich's symphonies, with their struggles, their shadowed or ambiguous triumphs, their sense of an individual against an implacable force, are the twentieth-century continuation of the Tchaikovskian symphonic drama. The question the Fifth Symphony raised, whether a triumphant ending is sincere or forced (Chapter 14), is one Shostakovich made central to his own work under political pressure. Tchaikovsky's symphonic legacy runs straight to him.

Through these three, Rachmaninoff, Stravinsky, Shostakovich, and many others, Tchaikovsky's hybrid art became one of the foundations of twentieth-century music, Russian and beyond. He is not a dead end or a period piece; he is a source.

### 20.4 The Soviet afterlife

Tchaikovsky's posthumous reputation took a strange turn under the Soviet Union, and understanding it is part of understanding why the honest picture of him is only now, in our own time, becoming clear.

The Soviet state adopted Tchaikovsky as a national treasure, the great Russian composer, performed constantly, taught in the conservatories, exported as a symbol of Russian cultural achievement. The Russian performance tradition of Chapter 19, Mravinsky and the rest, flourished under this official esteem. But the Soviet embrace came at a cost to the truth. The state suppressed the facts of Tchaikovsky's life that did not fit the image of a heroic national artist, above all his homosexuality, which Soviet biography systematically denied, hid, or explained away. The honor-court-suicide rumor (Chapter 21) and the truth of the marriage and the inner life were buried under an official portrait of a healthy, heterosexual, patriotic genius. Soviet Tchaikovsky scholarship, for all its musical seriousness, was constrained by what the state would permit to be said about the man.

So the twentieth-century world inherited a Tchaikovsky who was musically celebrated but biographically falsified: the great tunes and the great symphonies, performed magnificently, attached to a life story that had been edited for ideological purposes. The "sentimental Russian" caricature in the West and the "heroic Soviet genius" portrait in the East were both, in different ways, distortions, and both stood in the way of seeing the actual man.

### 20.5 The post-Soviet recovery

The opening of the Soviet archives after 1991, and the work of scholars willing to tell the truth, has made it possible, in our own time, to recover the honest Tchaikovsky. Alexander Poznansky's research (Chapters 8, 14, 21) established the facts of the sexuality and the inner life that the Soviet era had suppressed; Roland John Wiley's 2009 biography synthesized the modern, documented understanding; the full correspondence, including the von Meck letters and the family letters, became more freely available. The Tchaikovsky who emerges is neither the sentimental Russian of the Western caricature nor the heroic Soviet genius of the official portrait, but a specific, complicated, fully human artist: a homosexual man in a hostile society, a sufferer of nervous crises, a worker of immense discipline, a hybrid between Russia and the West, a master of every large form, whose openness of feeling was the surface of a structurally exact art.

This recovery is recent and ongoing, which is why Wiley could write, as Chapter 1 quoted, that Tchaikovsky's place in the canon is secure while his place in his own biography is still being revised. The music never needed rescuing; it has been loved continuously for over a century. What has needed rescuing is the truth of the man, from both the Western condescension and the Soviet falsification, and that rescue is the work of the scholarship this book has leaned on. To hear Tchaikovsky rightly today is to hear him freed from both caricatures: a great and serious composer, fully Russian and fully European, whose actual life, in all its difficulty and dignity, can at last be told.

### 20.6 The position, finally

Tchaikovsky's place is, then, a place between: between the nationalist Five and the Western mainstream, between Russia and Europe, between the open feeling that made him beloved and the structural rigor that made him great, between a life falsified by his admirers and a truth recovered by his scholars. He resolved none of these tensions by choosing a side; he lived in the middle of all of them, and the music is the record of that life in the middle. The doubleness is not a weakness to be explained away; it is the essence. He is the Russian composer the world adopted because he was also European; the serious master beloved as a melodist; the hybrid who became a source for Rachmaninoff and Stravinsky and Shostakovich; the man whose truth we are still learning to tell. That is his position, and it is a unique one in the history of music.

We have one hard chapter left before the book closes: the death, and the legend that has grown around it. Having understood who Tchaikovsky was, between Russia and the West, between feeling and rigor, between falsification and truth, we are ready to weigh, carefully and without a verdict, the disputed story of how he died.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Is being a hybrid usually a strength or a weakness for an artist? Tchaikovsky was both fully Russian and fully European, resolving the tension not by choosing a side but by living in the middle of it. What did he gain, and what might he have lost, by belonging fully to neither camp?"_
* _"Tchaikovsky's music traveled internationally faster than the nationalist Mighty Five's because it was 'Russian enough to be exotic and Western enough to be familiar.' Is there a tension between art that speaks to a specific culture and art that travels universally? Can you think of other artists whose 'in-between' quality helped them reach the world?"_
* _"The chapter traces Tchaikovsky's legacy to Rachmaninoff (the melodic line), Stravinsky (the classical side and the symphonic ballet), and Shostakovich (the tragic symphony). If you know any of these composers, can you hear the Tchaikovsky in them? Which inheritance, the lyrical, the classical, or the tragic-symphonic, seems strongest to you?"_
* _"The Soviet state celebrated Tchaikovsky's music while suppressing the truth of his life, producing a 'heroic Soviet genius' portrait as distorting as the Western 'sentimental Russian' caricature. Why might a state want to falsify the biography of a composer it admired? What does it cost us to inherit great music attached to a false life story?"_
* _"The honest Tchaikovsky, the chapter says, is only now being recovered, after the Soviet archives opened. Why does it matter to tell the truth about a long-dead composer's life when the music itself never needed rescuing? Does knowing the real man change how you hear the music, and should it?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This synthesis chapter invites a legacy-listening exercise. If you can, sample these connections:

  1. **Tchaikovsky and Rachmaninoff.** Hear a Tchaikovsky melody (the Romeo and Juliet love-theme) and then a Rachmaninoff one (from a piano concerto). Listen for the shared long arching line, the rich harmony, the Russian melancholy.

  2. **The Russian substance, Western form.** In any symphony, hear the folk-tinged Russian melody set inside a Western sonata structure. This is the hybrid in action.

  3. **The symphonic ballet's legacy.** Recall how _Swan Lake_ and _The Sleeping Beauty_ made ballet music symphonic, and consider that Stravinsky's _Rite of Spring_ descends from this idea.

  4. **The tragic symphony's legacy.** Recall the shadowed triumphs of the Fourth and Fifth, and the honest darkness of the Sixth, and consider how Shostakovich made the question of sincere-versus-forced triumph central to his own symphonies.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair a Tchaikovsky work with a work by one of his heirs, the Romeo and Juliet love-theme with a Rachmaninoff slow movement, or a Tchaikovsky symphony finale with a Shostakovich one. The comparison, about half an hour, lets you hear Tchaikovsky as a source rather than an endpoint, the founder of a line that runs deep into the twentieth century. It is the best way to feel his historical importance, not just his beauty.

**Read** : the discussions of Tchaikovsky's position and legacy in Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (2009), and, for the recovery of the honest biography, Alexander Poznansky's _Tchaikovsky: The Quest for the Inner Man_ (1991), which opened the modern, truthful account of the life. For the Soviet reception and its distortions, general histories of Russian and Soviet music discuss the official embrace and the suppression. Reading these will complete your understanding of who Tchaikovsky was, and prepare you for the careful, verdict-free treatment of his death in Chapter 21.

---

## Chapter 21: The Death of Tchaikovsky — November 1893

Nine days after he conducted the premiere of his Sixth Symphony, Pyotr Ilyich Tchaikovsky was dead, at fifty-three. The official cause was cholera, the same disease that had killed his mother when he was fourteen. And around that death has grown the most persistent and the most contested legend in all of Tchaikovsky biography: the story that he did not die of cholera at all, but was forced to kill himself by a secret "court of honor" to avoid the exposure of his homosexuality. This chapter confronts that dispute directly, and it does so in the way this book has practiced from the beginning: by laying out the positions, weighing the sources, and declining to deliver a verdict, because the dispute itself, and the question of how we know what we claim to know about a man's death, is the real material.

This is the hardest chapter in the book, and the one where the source-discipline we have practiced since Chapter 2, with the Tolstoy story, the cold-water immersion, the Rubinstein rejection, matters most. Everything in those earlier chapters was preparation for this: the habit of asking who is telling a story, on what evidence, with what interest. We bring that habit, now, to the most consequential question in the biography.

### 21.1 The documented sequence

Begin with what is least disputed. In the autumn of 1893, St. Petersburg was suffering an outbreak of cholera, a waterborne disease spread by contaminated water, much feared and often fatal. Tchaikovsky was in the city, in good spirits by most accounts, having just conducted the Sixth Symphony's premiere on October 16 / 28 and making plans for the future. Around October 20 (Old Style), he is reported to have drunk a glass of unboiled water, a dangerous thing to do during a cholera epidemic, when boiling water was the standard precaution. He fell ill with symptoms consistent with cholera, was attended by doctors, and after several days of severe illness died on October 25 / November 6, 1893, at the age of fifty-three. The recorded cause was cholera.

(A reminder on the dates, from Chapter 1: nineteenth-century Russia used the Julian calendar, twelve days behind the Western Gregorian. October 25 Old Style is November 6 New Style. Both dates refer to the same day.)

This is the account given in Modest Tchaikovsky's biography (1900-1902) and supported by contemporary documents and the recollections of those present. It is a plain, if grim, story: a careless glass of water during an epidemic, a fatal infection, a death from the disease that had also taken his mother. For most of the twentieth century, this was the accepted account.

### 21.2 The three positions

The cause of Tchaikovsky's death is genuinely disputed among serious people, and the honest thing is to present the dispute as a dispute, in three positions, without telling you which is true. This is the three-position observational frame that the §5.T1 principle of this book requires.

#### Position one: cholera (the contemporary account and Modest's biography)

The first position is the traditional one: Tchaikovsky died of cholera, contracted from contaminated water during the St. Petersburg epidemic. The evidence is the contemporary record, the doctors' accounts, Modest Tchaikovsky's biography, and the recollections of those present, all of which describe an illness with cholera's symptoms and a death from the disease. On this account, the death was a tragic accident, a careless glass of unboiled water during an epidemic, and there is nothing more sinister to explain. The disease was real, common, and frequently fatal; Tchaikovsky's death from it, while shocking in its suddenness, was the kind of thing that happened in a cholera year.

The weakness critics have pointed to in this position is that its central sources, especially Modest's biography, had reasons to present a clean account, and that some details of the reported illness and the handling of the body have been questioned. But it remains the position supported by the contemporary documentary record.

#### Position two: the honor-court suicide (the Orlova hypothesis)

The second position is the sensational one, and it has a specific origin. In 1979, the émigré music scholar Alexandra Orlova, who had left the Soviet Union, published a hypothesis, first in a Russian-language émigré journal and then, in expanded form, in an English-language article, "Tchaikovsky: The Last Chapter," in the journal _Music & Letters_ in 1981. Orlova's thesis was that Tchaikovsky did not die of cholera but was driven to suicide. According to the account she reported, Tchaikovsky's homosexuality was about to be exposed, allegedly through a complaint that threatened to reach the Tsar, and a secret "court of honor" of his former classmates from the School of Jurisprudence convened and pressured him to take his own life to avoid a public scandal that would disgrace them all. He is said to have taken poison, arsenic, whose symptoms can resemble those of cholera, and the cholera diagnosis was a cover.

Orlova based the hypothesis largely on oral testimony she said she had received, second- and third-hand accounts passed down and reported to her, rather than on contemporary documents. The thesis caused a sensation, fit the late-twentieth-century interest in the suppressed truth of Tchaikovsky's sexuality, and entered popular culture, where it is often repeated as established fact.

#### Position three: the refutation (Poznansky's re-examination)

The third position is the modern scholarly rebuttal. Alexander Poznansky, the scholar whose research established the honest account of Tchaikovsky's sexuality (Chapters 8, 20), examined the suicide hypothesis in detail, above all in his 1996 book _Tchaikovsky's Last Days: A Documentary Study_. Poznansky argued that the cholera account is, in fact, well supported by the contemporary documentary and medical evidence, and that the honor-court-suicide thesis rests on unverifiable hearsay, contains internal inconsistencies and implausibilities, and does not survive close scrutiny. He pointed to the difficulties of the suicide story: the implausibility of so elaborate a secret conspiracy, the inconsistencies in the various oral accounts, the strength of the documentary evidence for an actual cholera illness. Poznansky's conclusion is that the cholera diagnosis is the historically supported one, and that the suicide hypothesis, however dramatically appealing, is not established by the evidence.

Importantly, Poznansky is not a scholar with an interest in sanitizing Tchaikovsky; he is the very scholar who insisted on the truth of the sexuality that the Soviet era suppressed. His rejection of the suicide thesis is not a denial of who Tchaikovsky was; it is a judgment about what the evidence actually supports regarding how he died.

### 21.3 Why no verdict

This book does not tell you which position is correct, and the refusal is principled, not evasive. Here is why.

First, the question may not be decidable on the available evidence. The contemporary record supports cholera; the suicide thesis rests on hearsay that cannot be verified; the modern scholarship favors cholera but cannot absolutely disprove a secret suicide that would, by its nature, have been concealed. A responsible account acknowledges that we may simply not be able to know with certainty, and that pretending to a certainty the evidence does not support, in either direction, is a failure of honesty.

Second, the dispute itself is the historical material, and it is more instructive than any verdict would be. The story of how a sensational hypothesis arose, spread, entered popular culture, and was then subjected to scholarly scrutiny is a case study in how we come to believe things about the past, and in the difference between a vivid story and a verified fact. The whole book has been training you in this difference, from the Tolstoy weeping (Chapter 2) through the cold-water immersion (Chapter 8) and the Rubinstein rejection (Chapter 12). The death is the supreme case: a story so dramatic, so well-suited to a particular narrative about a tragic gay genius, that it spread despite resting on hearsay, and that required careful scholarship to weigh. Learning to hold this dispute as a dispute, without rushing to the more dramatic answer, is worth more than being handed a verdict.

Third, the verdict-rush distorts the music. As Chapter 14 argued, the temptation to read the Sixth Symphony as a suicide note depends on first accepting the suicide story; if you let the honor-court thesis settle in your mind as fact, you will hear the Sixth as a coded farewell, which falsifies it. By holding the death-question open, you protect the music from being conscripted into a biographical legend. The Sixth is a great symphony that ends in honest darkness for compositional reasons (Chapter 14); it is not made greater or more meaningful by being attached to a contested suicide.

### 21.4 The pattern, completed

The death-dispute completes a pattern this book has traced from its second chapter. Tchaikovsky's life attracts vivid, dramatic stories, the weeping Tolstoy, the river suicide attempt, the brutal Rubinstein rejection, the honor-court death, and each of these stories is more vivid than the documented record warrants, each owes much to a limited or interested source, and each has been polished by retelling until it is repeated as fact. The biographer's task, and the careful reader's, is to feel the pull of the dramatic story while refusing to mistake it for the established truth.

The death is the hardest case because the stakes are highest and the story is most charged. The honor-court suicide is a story that "wants" to be true for a certain modern sensibility: it makes Tchaikovsky a martyr to homophobic persecution, it gives his death a terrible meaning, it turns the Sixth Symphony into a suicide note. These are powerful attractions, and they are exactly why the story must be weighed with extra care, not less. The scholarship, in Poznansky's work, has done that weighing and favors cholera; the contemporary record supports cholera; the suicide thesis rests on hearsay. But the question remains formally open, and this book leaves it open, having shown you the positions and the evidence, because that is what honesty requires and because the open question teaches more than a forced answer.

### 21.5 What is not in dispute

End with what is certain, against the noise of the dispute. Tchaikovsky died in St. Petersburg in early November 1893, at fifty-three, nine days after conducting the premiere of his greatest symphony. He died at the height of his international fame, a celebrated artist, not a broken man, whatever the manner of his death. His homosexuality, long suppressed by the Soviet state and sensationalized by the suicide legend, is a fact of his life to be acknowledged honestly and without melodrama, as Chapter 20 described. And his music, the symphonies and concertos and ballets and operas this book has walked through, was complete: he had written the Sixth Symphony, the last and in some ways the greatest of his works, and conducted its first performance, before he died. Whatever killed him, he died having finished his work.

That is the dignity to hold onto, against both the Soviet falsification and the suicide melodrama: a great composer, at the height of his powers and fame, who had just given the world his final masterpiece, and who died, by cholera or by some contested other means, with his work complete. The disputes about the manner of his death matter for the truth, and we have weighed them honestly. But they do not touch the achievement, and they do not change the music. We turn, in the final chapter, from the death to the legacy, and to where you go from here.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"This chapter presents three positions on Tchaikovsky's death, cholera, honor-court suicide, and the scholarly refutation, and refuses to deliver a verdict. Why might refusing a verdict be more honest than choosing the most likely answer? Is there a difference between 'we don't know for certain' and 'all positions are equally likely'? How would you weigh the three?"_
* _"The honor-court-suicide story rests largely on oral testimony reported decades later, while the cholera account rests on contemporary documents. The suicide story is far more dramatic and 'wants' to be true for a modern sensibility. How should the dramatic appeal of a story affect how skeptically we examine it? Why might a charged story require more scrutiny, not less?"_
* _"Alexander Poznansky, who rejects the suicide thesis, is the same scholar who established the truth of Tchaikovsky's suppressed homosexuality. Why does it matter that the person debunking the suicide story is not someone trying to 'sanitize' Tchaikovsky? How does the source of a claim, and that source's interests, affect how we weigh it?"_
* _"The chapter argues that accepting the suicide story leads people to hear the Sixth Symphony as a coded suicide note, which falsifies the music. Have you been tempted to hear the 'Pathétique' that way? How does holding the death-question open protect the music from being conscripted into a legend?"_

The death is the hardest case. ### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This chapter has no anchor work, but the Sixth Symphony hovers over it. Return to the Sixth Symphony's finale (Chapter 14) and listen to it once more, with this chapter's caution in mind:

  1. **Hear the finale as music, not as a death.** Listen to the _Adagio lamentoso_ and try to hear it purely as the resolution of Tchaikovsky's lifelong question about sincere versus forced triumph (Chapter 14), not as a suicide note.

  2. **Notice the temptation.** Notice how strongly the knowledge that Tchaikovsky died nine days after conducting this music pulls you toward hearing it as a farewell. Feel the pull, and then set it aside.

  3. **Hold the death-question open.** As you listen, hold in mind that the manner of his death is genuinely disputed, and that the symphony was a considered artistic decision, not a coded message.

  4. **Hear the completeness.** Hear the finale as the last word of a complete body of work, the achievement finished, whatever killed its composer nine days later.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : there is no new listening for this chapter; instead, sit with the Sixth Symphony's finale one final time, holding the chapter's caution. The exercise is not musical analysis but a discipline of hearing: to hear the music as music, resisting the legend, is the hardest and most important kind of listening this book asks of you.

**Read** : Alexander Poznansky's _Tchaikovsky's Last Days: A Documentary Study_ (Oxford, 1996), the essential modern examination of the death, which lays out the documentary evidence and the case against the suicide thesis in full. For the other side, Alexandra Orlova's 1981 article "Tchaikovsky: The Last Chapter" in the journal _Music & Letters_ states the honor-court hypothesis. Reading both, the hypothesis and its refutation, is the best way to weigh the dispute for yourself, and it is a model of how a careful reader holds a contested historical question open rather than rushing to the more dramatic answer.

## Chapter 22: Where to Go Next — and the Tchaikovsky Recordings to Live With

You have walked through about fifty Tchaikovsky works across fifty-three years of a life, from the Andante cantabile a workman once hummed to the Sixth Symphony that fades into silence. You can now recognize his voice, the long arching melody, the recurring motto, the dramatic orchestral color, the folk-tinged Russian feeling inside Western forms. You can place each major work in the arc of the life: the formation, the Moscow years, the 1877 crisis and the von Meck patronage, the freelance decade, the late peak, the death. You know what is documented and what is disputed. This final chapter sends you onward: it gives you a starter list, a reading list, a word on the documentaries, and a reflection on where this volume sits in the _Close Listening_ series and where your own listening goes from here.

This is also the close of the first seven-volume single-composer thread of _Close Listening_ , the thread that began with Beethoven and ran through Mozart, Chopin, Bach, Schubert, and Brahms to Tchaikovsky. So this chapter looks both forward, to your continuing listening, and back, to the journey the series has been.

### 22.1 Your first ten Tchaikovsky listens

If a friend asked you where to start with Tchaikovsky, here is the list to give, ten works (or movements) that open the whole catalogue, drawn from the chapters of this book. Listen to them in roughly this order, and you will have the essential Tchaikovsky in your ear.

  1. **The Andante cantabile from String Quartet No. 1** (Chapter 2), seven minutes, the gentlest entrance: the melodic gift at chamber scale.
  2. **Romeo and Juliet, Fantasy-Overture** (Chapter 4), the love-theme and the dramatic mastery: the first great masterwork.
  3. **The white-swan adagio from _Swan Lake_** (Chapter 7), the symphonic ballet and the supreme love-music.
  4. **Symphony No. 4, first movement and finale** (Chapter 11), the Fate symphony: the tragic symphonist arrives.
  5. **Piano Concerto No. 1, first movement** (Chapter 12), the famous opening and the heroic concerto.
  6. **Violin Concerto, first movement** (Chapter 13), the soaring concerto as melodic vehicle.
  7. **The Tatiana Letter Scene from _Eugene Onegin_** (Chapter 10), the intimate operatic tragedy.
  8. **Symphony No. 5, slow movement** (Chapter 14), the great horn melody: the lyrical Tchaikovsky at his peak.
  9. **The Dance of the Sugar Plum Fairy and the Pas de Deux from _The Nutcracker_** (Chapter 16), the orchestral color-magic.
  10. **Symphony No. 6 "Pathétique," finale** (Chapter 14), the lament that fades to silence: the deepest and last.

Ten works, perhaps three hours of listening, and you have met the whole composer: the chamber miniaturist, the dramatic symphonist, the balletmaster, the concerto-writer, the opera composer, the colorist, the tragedian. Everything else in the catalogue opens off these ten.

### 22.2 Going deeper into the catalogue

When the ten have become familiar, the catalogue rewards further exploration. By genre:

* **The symphonies** : beyond the Fourth, Fifth, and Sixth, hear the early "Winter Dreams" (Chapter 3) and "Little Russian" (Chapter 6), and discover the _Manfred Symphony_ (Chapter 13), the great underrated "seventh."
* **The ballets** : hear all three complete, _Swan Lake_ , _The Sleeping Beauty_ (Chapter 15), _The Nutcracker_ (Chapter 16), not just the suites; the complete ballets are symphonic dramas.
* **The operas** : beyond _Eugene Onegin_ , hear _The Queen of Spades_ (Chapter 17), the dark counterpart, and discover the gentle one-act _Iolanta_.
* **The concertos and concertante works** : beyond the two famous concertos, hear the Variations on a Rococo Theme for cello (Chapter 8) and, if you wish, the Second and Third Piano Concertos.
* **The orchestral works** : the Serenade for Strings (Chapter 13), the _Capriccio Italien_ , the programmatic _Francesca da Rimini_ and _Hamlet_ ; and, for spectacle, the _1812 Overture_ (Chapter 13), heard with the knowledge that Tchaikovsky thought little of it.
* **The chamber music** : the three string quartets (Chapter 2), the Piano Trio in memory of Nikolai Rubinstein (Chapter 13), and the _Souvenir de Florence_ string sextet, the underrated private Tchaikovsky.
* **The songs** : Tchaikovsky wrote about a hundred songs, a body of intimate, lyrical work little known outside Russia, worth discovering if the operas leave you wanting more of his vocal writing.

### 22.3 The recordings to live with

Drawing together the Primary Recordings and alternatives from across the book, here is a small, reliable library, all of it time-tested or widely reviewed (the recording-hallucination safeguard applies throughout):

* **Late symphonies (4, 5, 6)** : Yevgeny Mravinsky / Leningrad Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960), the Russian-school benchmark. For a Western alternative, Herbert von Karajan / Berlin Philharmonic (DG).
* **Complete symphonies** : Mariss Jansons / Oslo Philharmonic (Chandos) or Mikhail Pletnev / Russian National Orchestra (DG).
* **The ballets** : André Previn / London Symphony (EMI) or Antal Doráti (Philips/Mercury); Valery Gergiev / Mariinsky (Philips) for the Russian theatrical sound.
* **Piano Concerto No. 1** : Sviatoslav Richter / Karajan / Vienna Symphony Orchestra (DG, 1962); Van Cliburn / Kondrashin (RCA) or Martha Argerich as alternatives.
* **Violin Concerto** : Jascha Heifetz / Reiner / Chicago (RCA, 1957); David Oistrakh or Itzhak Perlman as alternatives.
* **Eugene Onegin** : Georg Solti / Royal Opera House (Decca) or James Levine / Dresden (DG); Rostropovich's Bolshoi account for the Russian sound.
* **The Queen of Spades** : Valery Gergiev / Kirov (Philips).
* **Rococo Variations and chamber works** : Mstislav Rostropovich / Karajan for the Rococo Variations (DG); a Borodin Quartet set for the string quartets.

This is enough for a listening life. Add to it by comparison, hearing a second recording of a work you love, as Chapter 19 recommended, and by following the recording-comparison critics for current well-reviewed releases.

### 22.4 The reading list

For the life and the music, the modern scholarship this book has leaned on:

* **Roland John Wiley,_Tchaikovsky_** (Oxford, Master Musicians series, 2009), the contemporary single-volume gold standard, the first book to read for the documented, balanced modern account.
* **Anthony Holden,_Tchaikovsky: A Biography_** (1995), the most readable narrative life, vivid and humane.
* **David Brown,_Tchaikovsky: A Biographical and Critical Study_** (four volumes, 1978-1991), the comprehensive scholarly biography, for the deepest dive into the music and the life.
* **Alexander Poznansky,_Tchaikovsky: The Quest for the Inner Man_** (1991), the essential modern study of the inner life and the sexuality, which opened the honest account, and his _Tchaikovsky's Last Days_ (1996), the documentary examination of the death (Chapter 21).
* **Modest Tchaikovsky, the brother's biography** (1900-1902), available in English through Rosa Newmarch's 1906 abridgment, _The Life and Letters of Peter Ilich Tchaikovsky_ , the primary source, to be read with awareness that Modest was shaping a posthumous image (a caution this book has practiced throughout).
* **The von Meck correspondence** : the English selection edited by Edward Garden and Nigel Gotteri, translated by Galina von Meck, _'To my best friend': Correspondence between Tchaikovsky and Nadezhda von Meck, 1876-1878_ (Oxford, 1993), which covers only the first two years; the full correspondence exists in the Russian edition.
* For the ballets specifically, **Roland John Wiley,_Tchaikovsky's Ballets_** (Oxford), the definitive study of the three ballets and the Petipa collaboration (Chapters 15, 16).

### 22.5 A word on the documentaries and the films

There are documentary films on Tchaikovsky, including BBC and other broadcast treatments, of varying reliability; approach any of them with the source-discipline this book has taught, checking dramatic claims against the scholarship. One film deserves a specific caution: Ken Russell's _The Music Lovers_ (1971), a feature film about Tchaikovsky's life, is a work of dramatic invention, vivid and stylized, that takes large liberties with the facts and leans into the sensational. It is not a reliable biography; it is a filmmaker's fantasia. Enjoy it, if you wish, as a film, but do not take it as a source. The same caution applies to any dramatization: the life of Tchaikovsky has attracted melodrama, and the careful listener separates the music and the documented life from the legends, the films, and the fantasies.

### 22.6 The close of the single-composer thread

This volume is the seventh single-composer deep dive in the _Close Listening_ series, and with it the first thread of that series, the great composers from Beethoven onward, comes to a close. The thread has taken you through Beethoven's heroic struggle, Mozart's perfect speech, Chopin's piano poetry, Bach's architecture, Schubert's song, Brahms's autumnal mastery, and now Tchaikovsky's melody, color, and tragic symphonic drama. Each volume has used the same four-section listening loop, the same close attention to specific works, the same care about sources and legends. Each has tried to take a great composer seriously, beyond the caricatures, and to give you the tools to hear his voice.

Tchaikovsky is a fitting close to the thread, because he is the composer who most directly confronts the prejudice this whole series has worked against: the prejudice that immediately appealing music must be shallow, that openness of feeling is the enemy of seriousness, that the beloved cannot also be the great. Tchaikovsky is the most beloved of all the composers in the thread, and the most often patronized, and the deepest lesson of this volume is that his accessibility and his greatness are not in tension. The long arching melodies that make him beloved are the surface of an art that is structurally exact, emotionally precise, and historically pivotal. To hear that, to hear the rigor inside the beauty, is to have learned the central thing this series has tried to teach.

### 22.7 Where your listening goes from here

The listening does not end with the book. You have the ten starter works, the deeper catalogue, the recordings, the reading. What remains is the practice: to keep listening closely, to hear the same work in different performances, to follow the threads from Tchaikovsky to his heirs (Rachmaninoff, Stravinsky, Shostakovich, Chapter 20) and back to his sources (Glinka, Mozart, the Italian opera he loved), to let one composer open onto a whole tradition. Tchaikovsky is a door into Russian music, into the Romantic orchestra, into the symphonic ballet, into the question of how feeling and structure can be one. Walk through it.

And keep the habits this book has taught: listen for the specific thing, the melody, the motto, the color, the folk tune, the harmonic catch; set up each listen and meet yourself afterward to talk about what you heard; weigh the stories against the evidence; resist the legends and trust your own ear. These habits will serve you with any composer, in any tradition. They are the real gift of close listening, and Tchaikovsky, the most generous of composers, is a wonderful teacher of them.

The friend who has walked you through these fifty works now steps back. The music remains, and it is inexhaustible. Go and listen.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter offers ten starter works as the essential Tchaikovsky. If you had to cut the list to five, which would you keep, and why? What does your choice reveal about which side of Tchaikovsky, the lyrical, the tragic, the dramatic, the colorful, you value most?"_
* _"Having gone through the whole book, can you now hear rigor inside the beauty in a way you could not before? Many assume that immediately appealing music must be shallow, that accessibility and greatness pull against each other. Does Tchaikovsky bear that assumption out, or refute it, and what changed in your own listening across these chapters?"_
* _"Ken Russell's film _The Music Lovers_ and various documentaries take dramatic liberties with Tchaikovsky's life. Why is it worth being careful about dramatizations even when they are entertaining? How would you decide what to trust in a film or documentary about a composer's life?"_
* _"This volume closes a seven-composer thread (Beethoven through Tchaikovsky). If you have heard others in the series, where does Tchaikovsky sit among them for you? What does he do that none of the others does, and what does he share with them?"_
* _"The chapter ends by urging you to keep the habits of close listening: listen for the specific thing, weigh the stories against the evidence, trust your own ear. Which of these habits has been hardest for you, and which has changed your listening most? How will you carry them to the next composer you explore?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This closing chapter's exercise is to make the starter list your own. Over the coming weeks:

  1. **Listen to the ten starter works** (§22.1) in order, one or two an evening, doing the full four-section loop on each: set up the listen, hear the work, attend to the specific things, reflect afterward.

  2. **Choose your favorite** and hear it in two contrasting recordings (Chapter 19), discovering how much performance shapes meaning.

  3. **Follow one thread to its source or its heir** : from a Tchaikovsky melody to a Rachmaninoff one, or from his love of Mozart back to a Mozart work, hearing Tchaikovsky as part of a tradition.

  4. **Pick one work to live with for a month** , returning to it again and again, until it is wholly yours. That is what close listening is finally for: not to sample many works once, but to know a few works deeply, for a lifetime.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : begin the listening life this book has been preparing you for. Take the ten starter works and, over a month, make them familiar; then move into the deeper catalogue, genre by genre. There is no rush; Tchaikovsky's music is inexhaustible, and a lifetime of close listening will not use it up.

**Read** : start with Roland John Wiley's _Tchaikovsky_ (2009), the single best book for the documented modern account, and let it lead you to the others on the reading list as your interest deepens. And then read about the composers Tchaikovsky leads to, Rachmaninoff, Stravinsky, Shostakovich, and the ones he came from, Glinka, Mozart, letting one composer open onto the whole tradition. The reading, like the listening, is a lifetime's pleasure. This book has been an invitation; the rest is yours.
